LADIES' LETTER.

My DEAD. NELLIE,

I am glad to tell you th:-,A several new styles in blcrises have lately p.a in an appearance amongst us. Perhaps the most charming is a loose bodice of crepe de chine or fine muslin, worked here and there with tiny flowerets in gold thread or silk. The graceful folds and draping of this blouse depend on the more or less skilful adjustment of it. It is slightly open at the throat, tied across with narrow black Übe ribbon, whilst at the waist it is held in place by a broad soft eeinture of crepe de chine, knotted at one side ; the elbow sleeves are finished with a deep frill. For simple morning wear, the batiste or taffetas shirt, sailor shape, with two, or perhaps three, collars, turning back from a tucked muslin chemistte, and a knotted sailor tie in front, seems the kind most in vogue.

In millinery matters another radical change that appears to be impending is that of wearing the hat on the back of the head instead of shading the forehead. Also, hats seem to be growing very tall again, and there is some reason to fear that, should they not be headed back, wd may return to the very inartistic exaggeration of some six years since, when the hat was so very high that ladies of little height appeared to have their heads in the centre of the body, measuring from the waistline to th, topmost feather of the head- gear. Lightness is a characteristic of the fashion- able headgear. Tulle, chiffon, and feathers are all on the topmost wave of favour, and later on gauze ribbons will supersede glace. Roses made of silk chiffon and coloured gauzes of the most diaphanoux texture are used as substitutes for the ordinary artificial flowers.

I saw a very stylish tailor-made gown o&zrial out in mastic cloth. The smart little coat has a collar of the printed panne. A pretty waiatcoat of the same material is disclosed when the coat is thrown open. A fitted swathed band of dull blue silk encircles the waist, and a stylish toque in net of a blue to match, trimmed round one side with e handsome black oArich feather secured by a bril- liant and turquoise buckle completes the toilette.

Who long, clinging skirts that are fashionabti just now are most unsuitable for windy weather. They absolutely coil like cobras round the limbs and render them helpless. Most of the ladies' tailors are willing to supply sensible, short-skirted costumes to their customers, and these of a com- paratively inexpensive kind. The skirts are a good inch off the ground all round, and nothing can be neater or more appropriate to such weather as we are now having, and of which we may ex- pect a continuance, in some degree, right up to the end of May. The east wind generally lasts, off and on, "till May is out:" thereby justifying the old proverb that warns us against. doffing warm clothing in language that once was in the mouths of the cultured but has now become in- elegant. The effects of the popular craze for khaki 'are to be seen even in the fancy-work shops, where all kinds of articles in the favoured hue, with or without touches of scarlet, are being shown ready prepared for working. The simplest type of khaki-embroidery has for its foundation light brown linen on which is traced a bold conventional design intended to be worked over in satin-stitch with khaki cotton or flax-thread, and finally out- lined with scarlet stem-stitching. This embroidery can be satisfactorily applied to cushions, blotters, work-bags, and many other things, the lining, where one is necessary, being of scarlet silk and any cord, ribbon, or gimp trimmings of the two colours mingled.

Your loving JNYNIZ.

HOW TO MAKE USE OF THE SUN

Sun Baths for Strength.

The following notes have beEn sent us by a hygienist of note, and while publishing his notes we must say some of his theories are far fetched, but the principles laid down are good. One of the mot wonderful hygienic agents ever discovered is the ray of the sun, the various potencies of which, as applied in the service of human need and luxury, have ben carefully studied by a few investigators. Inasmuch as the sun with its emanations is the supreme factor in terrestrial life,it is to be regretted that the public as a whole are not more familiar with the leading principles of what is known as chromopathy, which can be applied without expert knowledge. And perhaps if the public were better informed as to what the sun can do for them, some of our housewives would not be o fearful of faded carpets ; or, rather, they would look upon that as one of the necessary concomitants of healthfu It is a matter of record that the nude or semi- nude races, whose bodies are largely exposed to the solar influence,are almost exempt from the diseases SO common to civilsatioo ; their strength, too, is proportibnably greater than ours; and they are said to compare vary favourpbly in the ability to acquire knowledge, developing wondered percep- tion and knowledge. 1111112M1 beings, like plants, require the direct light of the sun in order to attain a superior development. As a curious item , of information, it has been found that few, if any, suicides occur in rooms or offices on the sunny side of buildings, tragedies of this kind being almost ex- clusively confined to the shady side. When Takins, a House.

Steve was doubtless now wholly absorbed in gold- getting, that gold was doubtless his god, and he had 'ceased to love her. Else why should he be silent? Yes, he had gone out to win gold for her, but now it appeared the gold had more attraction for him than she herself had. He had ceased to love her. This thought was galling to her in the extreme. For a time she worried and fretted, then her proud spirit rose within her. She would not be miserable. She would not fret and worry, and trouble herself about Stephen Hart. No, indeed 1 She would let everyone see that she didn't care a rap about him. For the gossips had heard that Steve had ceased to correspond with Ella Rayburn, and they wagged their heads knowingly. Ralph Cartleigh learnt the news in good time from Ben. "I thought it would end that road," said Ben, sagely. "Lots o' these young chaps as goes out to forrin countries soon forgets thoose they left behind 'em. Absence may make th' heart grow fonder, as Solomon or some'dy else says; but it's oft fonder of the other woman or fellow, yo'll notice. I should no' wonder if Steve isn't wed to hauve-a- dozen nigger lasses, for I'm towd 'at it's th' fashion ,to have thoose many in these hot, forrin lands. By gow 1 I should think Steve'll have a rare sultry time of it when his little lot are all jawin' at him at once. One woman's quite enough for me ;lan' sometimes hoos one too many." Wakes time, with its welcome holidays, was in view again. Ralph Cartleigh, visiting the Rayburns, generously offered to defray the expenses of a week's outing at Blackpool for the widow and her daughter. Ella was averse to accepting the offer but her mother's entreaties prevailed. On the last Saturilay in August, therefore, Ella and her mother, escorted by the factory-master's son, journeyed to breezy Blackpool. Here, midst the whirl of merry life, in the gorgeous palaces of pleasure, in country drives and delightful sea-trips, in one continual round of bustling gaiety, the factory lass quite forgot her lover across the ocean, Steve, the Outlander, who at that time was in an agonised state of mind, waiting for the answering letters that would never come. Ella Rayburn was happy, happy in forgetfulness, forgetting the weary years of waiting, forgetting the toil of the mill, the incessant whirr-whirr of the loom, thinking only of the present, the glorious, thrilling, happy present, in which one man, her constant attendant, stood out conspicuous. Ralph Cartleigh had scored well in this, his latest move. He had spent his money lavishly, not stinting himself or his companions in the least. They occupied rooms in a first-class hotel, and lived in the best style. Ella, in common with most factory girls, was well-dressed; indeed, attired as fashionably as any of her feminine neighbours at table or in the street. As they took part in the North Pier "church parade," or "dress parade," as it might be more , correctly termed, Ralph declared that Ella "was the best-looking woman on the pier—from the dress or any other point of view." • The time passed all too quickly. A week was soon gone, and as the trio returned to smoky Dales- worth on Saturday night, Ella sighed with regret that the happy days were ended. "We can have many more such happy days, dear, if you but will it," whispered Ralph, amorously. He had not unduly pressed his suit during the past week. He had been content to observe the effect produced upon the •girl by his princely liberality. Day by day she had treated him with more affection—yea, more than that, Ralph assured himself; treated him with love. "Love, forsooth !" he said to himself. "Love she will give, and love she wants, and I will give it her—of a sort. I will be the good lover to her until such time as I see fit to throw aside the canting disguise. Love she shall have. I will emulate the true lover to the life, and thus con- found Steve, the valorous Outlander ! " So this mellow September night, as the deepening shadows fell over the land and the railway carriage became darkened, 1,4 e nestled closer to her as she sighed over the departed happy days. "We can have many more such happy days, if you will it, darling," he softly repeated. Widow Rayburn, the only other occupant of the first-class compartment, threw back her head against the soft cushions, and, in a moment, appeared to be sleeping soundly. "Say, Ella, shall all our days be happy ? " and he looked wistfully into the girl's fair face. She did not speak. She was endeavouring to reduce to order the chaotic state of her confused thoughts. Now, for a moment, the present and the past were both uppermost in her mind. Visions of a bright, stalwart, handsome young fellow, full of hope and courage, embarking to a far-off country to win wealth for her, flitted before her mental eye. Before her physical eye was the reality, a young, passionate, good-looking though rakish man, entreating her to give him all that she had promised to the other. This or that? She mentally balanced them. This one abroad who had sworn eternal love to her yet had neglected her; should it be this one, or ' that, the one who now sat by her side of whose true feelings towards her she had some doubts but whose actions and whose manners told their tale? This one or that, which should it be? She had not yet wholly lost faith in Steve though she tried to persuade herself that she had. On the other hand, she had not complete faith in the man beside her though she tried to believe that she had. "Ella, give me your reply, the reply I have been waiting for,so long." His voice roused her from her reverie. She hurriedly tried to determine on which side the scale should fall. It was useless. She was yet undecided. "I cannot give you a reply yet, Ralph. You must wait a little longer." That night as she lay abed reviewing the events of the past week, terminating in the journey home with the man who had so largely contributed to her happiness, Ella felt that she mighf well have given him the favourable reply he so urgently sought, and thus, once for all, have ended her own disquietude. But the morrow, a fair Sabbath morn, brought with it, besides the gleaming, radiant sunshine, a ray of searching light which illuminated the dark ways of the past. As Ella left her home early in the morning she met the postman, a cheery, talkative fellow, who at once accosted her. "Mornin', Mir Ella. Fine, grand, rosy, bonny EVERY WOMAN blushing mornin , just like yourself exceptin' th' blushes. This is South African weather, eh? We Should send two stamps for our 32 page Illustrated Boa could do with more like it. All's good 'at comes containing valuable information of how all Irregolaritie, front South Africa, eh ? Here's a letter for you. and Obstructions may be entirely avoided or removed Eh, these letters! Why, it's knocked all the by simple means. Recommended by eminent Physicians colour out of your rosy face. Eh, these letters, as the only Safe, Sure and Genuine Remedy. Never. 'specially th' love sort I Here it is. There's fails. Thousands of genuine unsolicited Testimonial somethin' good in that, I'll warrant, it's so from all over the Weal P. BLANCHARI Dalston-lace heavy." London.

Therefore, if you are renting a house, select one that is en a street running north and south, and then the sun will purify and vitalise the eastern rooms in the morning and the western rooms in the afternoon. Or, if you have a room to select, give the preference to some other than a northern ex- posure. The sun vitalises whatever it touches. If your drinking water could be sunned befoie using, a good draught of that morning and night would be coming about as near as you could get to drinking liquid light. Some people after drinking sunned water say they can feel its vivifying influence for hours afterwards. In fact,some people with whom ordinary water disagrees, find that they can drink the sunned water with pleasure and benefit. Brea! even, has been Isolated, and its nutritive value greatly enhanced. Colour is always and everywhere the measure of force ; and this is true not only of light as passed through glass mediums, but it is also the key to the potencies of minerals, herbs and fabrics. Almost everybody who has been in the habit of visiting curio shops has seen amber necklaces on sale. The wearing of these is said to give relief in certain affection, but only the student of colours knows whence their virtue is derived. Being of the yellow order, they give forth a mile animating influence to the nerves; and hence tend to overcome sluggish, congested conditions of the system.

Red is Animating.

Red, too,is warming and animating; but its vibra- tions are coarser, and it is more specifically adapted to the blood, upon which it exerts a great quicken- ing power. People with cold extremities ought to know this. Let them wear red stockings, and have their gloves lined with red; and if the nerves are , not too irritable, red underclothing will be It boon to them. But keep red away from the head, which is liable to become overheated; and needs tbe cool- ing, electrical influence of the blue, indigo or violet If you have a lining to your bat, it is an excellent thing to have it of blue, and a deep, dark blue at that—especially during the heated months of the year. These solar elements are not medicines in the sense of chemicals, but they are actual nerve-foods. and are taken up and incorporated into the nervola tissue. Blue-charged water is a marvellous remedy for inflamed, over-active conditions of the system It corresponds to an acid—like lemon juice for ex- ample—only it is a hundred times more refined, penetrating and pervasive. The red, yellow and orange rays, on the contrsry, are of the alkaline order. If mothers only knew it, thousands of babies' lives could be saved from the ravages of summer , complaints by the admini,tration of a little of this blue-charged water; and they could easily prepare it themselves.

===== 0002601_19000412_art0013.txt | included=true | included =====
Blue.

Of course, a centainer especially made for the purpose is the best, but an ordinary blue bottle bought from the chemist will meet the require- ments. Let the colour be of the deep, dark blue kind, as other blues admit more or less of the red rays, which give just the opposite effect. Fill this bottle with good, clear water, and let it stand in the full sun at least an hour before using. A longer time is better; and keep it standing in tke sun as much as possible while in use. Take a good swal- low or two at a time as a dose, which is equal to a wine-glassful, or two or three tablespoonfuls. A smaller quantity, says our informant, at a time will answer for infants. These solar food-medicines may seem to be too etbereal to produce much effect, but it is not so; and there is a marked difference between the quali- ties of the several rays. For instance, if you use a cork stopper for your blue bottle for charging water, after a little you will find that the cork. where it comes in contact with the water, will be- come quite darkly tinged; but this is not the case with tne cork when used in connection with the amber water. Again, blue-charged water is anti- septic. and will.keep sweat and fit for use for months ; but amber-charged water is not ansiteptic, and becomes infected with vegetable matter in the couroe of ten days or so, so that it should be re- newed every week when in use. Colour and Insanity.

The different rays also have a great influence on tbe nervous system and mentality. Severe cases of insanity have been cured by putting patients in rooms draped and lighted with the appropriate colour. A frenried, violent patient was quickly soothed by being ushered into a blue room; and a case of velancholic insanity was greatly heneated by putting the patient into a red room. Everybody from childhood up has heard about the rousing effect that red has on a bull, and how the Spanish bull-fighters excite their victims to fury by waving red 'cloths before them. The red vibrations, entering through the visual organs, actually do excite the blood in the brain; and in a case of melancholia,this quickening, enlivening influence is just what is wanted. Blue has just th 3 opposite effect, being quieting,sedative. Even a square of glass of the desired colour hung up in your sunny window will have an excellent effect, as the special colour potency given forth through it will permeate your room. some of the good results obtained in this way have been remark- able. Although the solar forces are so refined and exquisite, they are far reaching in their effects When concentrated upon the body by means of reflectors, they cure cancer, consumption, etc., and they are believed to be one of the few things that will eradicate scrofula and leprosy from the system. —Science Sifiinss.

"WOMEN'S CHAT."

Many versions are given of the reason which first in- I duced the Queen to visit Ireland. The one, that the idea was a sudden inaniration, and that it came upon the Royal Family and the Ministers like a thunder bolt seems to be the most popular, hut it is of course all nonsense. Tnere is no doubt that the question of the Queen's visit was discussed at the time when the Duke of Cennaught was appointed to the Command of the Forces, in succession to Lord Roberts. The Duke was anxious for the Queen to visit Ireland, and when the journey to the South of Europe was abandoned, tier Majesty decided to carry out the suggestion, which was , however, known to the Royal Family and several of the Cabinet Menisters,sorne time before the official announce- ment appeared in the papers. It is probable that the IQueen's residence in Ireland will be the commencemeet of more freqeent Royal visits in the future and it is net !unlikely that next year, either the Prince and Prineess of wales, or the Duke and Duchess of York, will reside in Dublin for a few weeks during the season. STOCKTON HEATH.

PAROCHIAL COMMITTEE.

-- - - A meeting of the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee was held in the National Schools on Thursday last week. There were present Messrs. Mason(in the chair),l'earson, Clarke, Whitley, Mc Intyre.Fairhurst, G Ashton (clerk), Swinton (surveyor), and Farrington (nuisance inspector.)

PRIVATE STRBET WORKS ACT. The Clerk reported that the Rural District Council had appointed Mr. Robert Garnett of Wildersco)l as sur- veyor to put in force the Private Streets Works Act in the streets recommended by the committee, viz.,Gaskell Street, 133 stock Street, and Leonard Street. He would act as surveyor to the Council for the work. The property owners, of course, would pay for his services in the same way as for the making of the streets. After they were made they became the property of the Rural District Council and they would have to keep them in order.

THS NIIMENIIING OF ROUSES. The Clerk said that at the last meeting he was in structed'to find eut how the numbering .4 house's was nroceededwith at the last date that the eel:m.llWe° did till, work. He found that in 1895 a committee was appointed to ascertain the number of houses in the streets and carry out the work. They aprarerl to have got the numbers from Messrs. Caldwell'. Limited, and Mr Frank Warburton bad done the work for them. The numbers cost £ll 23 Od, and the work a 23 71. It was the duty of the occupierc to number the houses accord- ing to section 65 of the Towns Improvement Clause Act. The Inspector was in*tructed to report on the number of houses requiring numbering. This was all the businetKof public interest.

THE .EXAMINER, THURSDAY, APRIL 12 1900

(Copyright.)

STEVE, THE OUTLANDER.

BY ARTHUR LAYCOCK, Author of "The Young Gaffer," "Seaside

Sketches," "Lancashire Sketches," &c.

Ella took the letter, murmuring tier tnanKs. "That's one I've brought you every month sine, February."

he started and gasped slightly. "That's—let's see, one every month for March, April, May, June, Joly, August, September—seven of 'em. I shall expect a Christmas-box at th' year end if I continue to brim). 'em at this rate," and laughing heartily the letter-carrier bustled off. Ella stood for a moment as one transfixed. The words just spoken cut into her heart and stayed its wild beating. Her face changed colour alternately from white to red, and the e'hand that held the letter ,trembled nervously. She turned with the intention of returning home, but had not gone many paces before she changed her mind, and, walking quickly, she hurried along the field path. "A letter from Steve?" she exclaimed, glancing at the familiar handwriting and tearing open the 'welepe. "And he has sent one every month, and the postman has brought it, but I've never received it. 0 mother! mother!" she cried, aloud, as the thought of her parent's culpability dawned upon her. "What have you done? What have you done ?"

She buried her face in her hands and sobbed bitterly, the hot tears falling on the letter she tightly clutched. "0 mother, what have you done! And what have I done, too! 0 Steve Steve! I have wronged you, I have wronged you!" "Unable to control her grief she hastened home- ward. The glory of the morn, the fair blue sky, the green grass at her feet were not seen by her. Her eyes were blinded with tears, black sorrow was at her heart and she was nigh unto distraction. Her appearance in the cottage in this distressed state astonished her mother greatly.

"Why, whatever's to do, lass, whatever's to do?" she cried as Ella fell into a chair and burst into loud lamentation. For reply the letter was thrown on to the table. "What's it mean? What's to do? What's this ? " cried Mrs. Rayburn in a breath. "Is some'dy dead, or what ? " She put on her spectacles and scanned the writing. Then the letter dropped to the floor from her nerveless hand. But quickly she recovered her composure. At first sight of Steve's letter she became much alarmed. Her guilty conscience told her there was trouble in store for her. But Widow Rayburn was not easily dismayed. She would brave it out.

"What does it mean, lass? What does it mean? Is Steve dead, or what ? "

"No, I don't know," sobbed the girl. "Oh, I wish I were dead! 0 mother, what have you done—what have you done ? " "What have I done ? " repeated Mrs. Rayburn, unembarrassed. "I've clone nowt serious as I know of. What do you think I've done?" . __

"Where are he letters that Steve has sent to me these past three months ? " "Where are they? I don't know. At least I- Who's bin tellin you owt about 'em?

"Jim, the postman," answered Ella, still weep- ing. "I met him just now and he says that he has brought letters here from South Africa every month. What have you done with them ? " "Well, I cannot hardly say," said Widow Rayburn, slightly ruffled at being thus cornered. "They've got put o' one side an' lost p'raps, an' getten burnt, an'—" "0 mother!" cried the girl, in sorrowful reproof. "How could you—how could you do it ? " "How could I? " exclaimed the mother, raising her voice in self-justification. "How could I, you ask? Because I'm a mother I could do it, that's why. Because I'd protect my dowter (daughter) against hersel'. Because I think so well of my dowter that I wouldn't see her throw herself away on a penniless gallivanter. That explains how I could do it, lass, an' if th' chance come I'd do it again." "You've no occasion to upbraid me," she con- tinued after pausing for breath. "I've done what I have done to benefit you. If you won't" do th' best for yoursel', I'll do it for you. How would you have been likely to have had that grand outin' to Blackpoo' if you'd bin thinkin' of Steve Hart an' had his big hopes an' promises in your mind? You wouldn't have gone to Blackpoo', I reckon?"

, "I certainly should not have gone," Ella answered, decisively. "Don't mention that matter to me again, mother, Oh, it is dreadful to think of now I"

"Aye, now," pitilessly sneered the parent. "Now when a letter comes from Afriky it's all altered. What were a grand, glorious outin', full of enjoyment, as you said it was—for I heard all , your talk in th' train, though I did pretend to be asleep—that grand, happy outin' is become a miserable thing to remember. I've no patience with you, Ella I You don't know your own mind two minutes together." The widow continued to rattle away as was her wont, but Ella heeded not her words. She had opened the letter and was now devouring its contents. Good fortune was mice again attend- ing Steve. Everything he touched prospered. Johannesburg, though not as flourishing as formerly, was in a fairly satisfactory state. Outlander grievances had not yet been settled, and there was still much disaffection and uneasiness; but, never- theless, work was going on and money was being made by a few fortunate individuals, amongst whom Steve included himself. He had been appointed chief storekeeper at the May Gold Mine at a hand- some salary. He sent twenty pounds in notes for Ella's holiday-money—" Wakes brass," he termed it. In a short time he would have many twenties to spare, then he would tell Ella of his plans for the future. The sight of crisp bank-notes had a most soothing effect on Widow Rayburn. These bits

of pape'r were as oil on troubled waters. "Oh, these are somethin' like," she said, taking up one of the notes and handling it affectionately. "If th' lad can manage to send-a two-three of these sort o' letters I shall have no objection to takin' 'em in an' lettin' you see 'em. He's doin' well, seemin'ly." Ella read the letter, and as she finished her mother came towards her with a tear in her eye, and in quavering voice said: "You'll forgive me, Ella, if I've done owt wrong? I did it all as I thought for th' best. I want you to come out right, that's all, as who you wed. Steve's not a bad sort, seemin'ly, an' p'raps after all, he'd make you th' best husband. You must please yoursel' which one you choose. Which- ever it is it'll be right for me, an' if it's Steve an' he wants you to go over yon, as I expect he -,411 do, well, you can go an' I'll not stand in th' way. I can go live with Aunt Emily for a time." "I don't think there'll be any need for that yet, mother," said Ella. "Steve doesn't say that he wants me to go to Africa, though to be sure he half hints that he may do later." "Well, any road will be right for me if you make a good match." Widow Rayburn, of course, could not refrain from making known their good fortune. Within a few days most of the neighbours had seen the precious notes sent by Steve; a few had even handled them. At Fernleigh Mill everyone had heard of them. Gossiping Ben, encountering the young manager, told him the news. "Yes, twenty pounds, he's sent in good, genuine crinklin' notes. If Steve isn't shovellin' gowd up, he's gotten' hold o' th' pappers 'at are worth as much. If that's bein' a oppressed Outlander,' I think I'll go an' be one. I could stand a rare lot of that sort of oppression. It would take a good many twenty pounds to oppress me. By gow But he must have getten' a better game on now nor shootin' an' puncin' th' Boers. He must have bin born' for gowd, eh? He'll be getten' too rich an' too proud soon to wear clogs, an' then he'll be lettin' some'dy else do th' Boer puncin' an' shootin' for him. Noan many fighters among th' twenty-pound nobs; they generally let th' twenty-bob a week chaps do that sort o' wark." "When did the letter come, Ben ? " asked "Q.a.lnh Cartleigh. th' beginnin' o' th' week, I under- starld ; but I cannot tell to a day. Ella will not say owt about it, but her mother makes up for't ; she's as good as a bellman. You can get owt out of her if you only go th' right road about it. Women is curious cattle, sometimes. They're in one mood one minute, an' another mood th' next, an' they're as fickle an' changeable as th' weather. I know !" Ralph Cartleigh had arrived at the same con- clusion concerning womankind in general, but re- garding one individual in particular. "I know how that letter will affect the fickle jade," he said to himself. "She will swear allegiance to Steve the Outlander now, and endeavour to throw me overboard, Curse it! And just when I felt certain of her. Ah, well, we shall see. This is only the beginning of the second act in our stirring melodrama, 'Steve, the Out- lander.' The villain will, yet come out triumphant." "When you've any particular news at any time, Ben, you can let me have it," he said to the ware- houseman who had been standing by intently vegardine him.

===== 0002601_19000412_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAPTER XV.

There was no need for Ella Rayburn to throw out bait, as suggested by her mother, to catch Ralph Cartleigh. He came uninvited to pay his attentions to the girl. Every week, now, he visited her at her home. Mrs. Rayburn gave him much encouragement, but for a time her daughter's attitude towards him was not altogether cordial. But the cunning Ralph knew how best to proceed to win his way. Ile had time and opportunity now to scheme and plan; he was not troubled much with other distracting affairs. Barrett had been finally and opportunely disposed of. Henry's money had come just in time to avert a catastrophe. At home, as at the mill, Ralph's conduct had been unimpeachable, so he assured himself. For a time he had given up gambling, given up excessive drinking, given up, too, the society of "lady friends" at Oldham and Manchester. Under these circumstances, he was better enabled to devote his whole thought and attention to the complete ensnarement of the object of his voluptuous desires. Ella- Rayburn must be won. He would win her! Fortunately for his purpose, Mrs. Rayburn con- tinued to successfully intercept the letters that came from South Africa. As Ella received no tidings from Stephen Hart, her feelings towards him under- went a gradual change. He was neglecting her. Perhaps, as her mother said, he had grown quite indifferent to her, was thinking only of himself and his affairs. From, Ben she had heard - that Transvaal affairs were becoming more settled again, business was brisker, and mines were being worked

once more.

===== 0002601_19000412_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
"All reet, Mestur Ralph," the sly fellow replied. "I understand. I've bin young mysel'," and bestowing a meaning wink on his superior, he waddled off, communing with himself on the matter. "If I can reckon th' business up Mestur Ralph's determined to have Ella Rayburn either by hook or crook. By crook, I should say, mysel, ' for Mestur Ralph's ways will not be over-straight if he's any opposition to meet. An' in Steve Hart he'll have a man to deal with, but th' worst on 't he's thousands o' miles away while Mestur Ralph's here on th' spot tryin' to catch th' lass at every turn. I can see he's on for havin' her. I saw th' look in his ee (eye) that night I met him an' her near Woodhouses, an' there were no mistakin' it. An' last week at Blackpoo' as I saw 'em whirlin' round an' dancing' in th' Tower ballroom I saw th' same look in his ee again. He's got both een on Ella Rayburn, an' he's near her every day, an' that counts for more nor all th' letters an twenty-pound notes in th' world 'at may be sent from Afriky. If I were Steve Po take a week-end ticket for England an' come o'er an' see how his lass, is gettin' on, or belike he'll find she's getten' off if he comes back too late. Absence makes th' heart grow fonder,' but as th' cynic said, 'fonder of the other fellow' ; an' I dare bet ten to one 'at t'other fellow, Ralph Cartleigh, will win th' lass."

As Ben saw the young manager conversing with Ella several times later, his opinion was strengthened. "He's gettin' there with both feet," he re- marked, "I can see th' lass flushin' an' blushin.' all th' time he's talkin' to her, an' you can bet owt 'at when a lass does that ruddy work she thinks a great deal 'o' th' chap 'at causes her to blush so. Eh, dear, dear, these wimmen, these wimmen To think 'at a lass will allow a chap to wheedle an' bamboozle her. to that extent is extryordinary. But wimmen are built that way, an we mus'nt find fault with th' Builder, though we feel sometimes 'at we'd like to alter th' plans 'at they're built on. If Ella goes on at this rate long, encouragin' our master's son—well, she'll either be Mrs. Ralph Cartleigh or else become another of th' broken strings to his long bow." To others, beside the warehouseman, Ella appeared to be coming more and more under Ralph Cartleigh's influence. Truth to tell, the girl was in a quandary. She desired now to be true to Steve; at the same time, also, she wanted to bz friendly with Ralph. She remembered the latter's many kindnesses. The Wakes holidays, presents of jewellery; his valour, too, in rescuing her nearly twelve months ago she recollected with gratitude. Try as she would she could not be distant or cold with him.

Cartleigh visited her frequently now, both at her work and in her home. As Christmas again drew nigh he ventured to propose that Ella should accompany him to a fashionable ball at Oldham, on Boxing Night. "Just as a friend," he said. "I cannot, I suppose, regard you as more than a friend yet." Ella would not then promise, but as the time drew nigh and a handsome gold necklet was sent to her by "A Friend," she decided to accede to his request. In the short interval intervening she busied herself in making preparations for the great event, and procured a fashionable ball dress and costly opera cloak. The greater portion of Steve's twenty pounds was expended on these latter. "Just for this once," said Ella, in self excuse, "I'll never go with Ralph any more. This I shall consider will amply repay him for all that he has done for me."

===== 0002601_19000412_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAPTER XVI.

The Oldham ball was a most fashionable affair. The elite of Cottontown and neighbourhood were present in considerable numbers. Ella, who had never previously attended such a society function, was somewhat overawed by the glamour and glitter of the magnificent scene as it first appeared to her astonished eyes. The superbly decorated hall, the glare and glitter of electric light, the shim- mering dresses of gaily-dressed women, the stylishly attired men, many in uniforms, all made up a picture which impressed her profoundly. Here were mayors, aldermen, justices of the peace and other notabilities whom she regarded with awe almost; people of superior clay to her humble class.

For a weaver lass to mingle with this fashionable throng seemed sacrilege. It was with the greatest difficulty that Ralph Cartleigh prevailed upon her to take part in the dancing. But once she was gliding over the beautifully polished floor, with Cartleigh's right arm clasped tightly about her slim waist, revelling in the flow of the waltz and the nicely modulated music of the band, she became herself again— natural, and at ease. Her partner gladly noted her evident enjoyment. Surely after this night's delight she would become subservient to him. Until the early hours of the morning the dancing went merrily on. By this time Ella had become weary and jaded. She was thankful that no mill work awaited her for a few days.

Cartleigh led her for the third time to the refreshment buffet, and ordered wine for her and spirits for himself. He had already partaken of too much liquor, and was in a half-maudlin state, though his speech was still coherent. "Thanks, I prefer coffee," said Ella; as the wine was placed before her.

"Oh, no, you musn't have such watery slops," said Cartleigh, taking the glass from the waiter who was about to bear it away. Observing her companion's condition and not desiring to do any- thing that would attract attention from the people in their vicinity, Ella sipped the wine, and im- mediately a dazed feeling came upon her. It was but momentary, and, as Cartleigh chaffed her, she drained the glass. "Another ? " he said.

she replied, "no more, thanks. I'm feeling dizzy again; no more." But another glass of wine was brought, and Cartleigh insisted upon her drinking it. Realising her danger, Ella glanced timidly about her. Seated at the tables near at hand were groups of ladies and gentlemen conversing together quietly. If she expostulated with her companion these people would overhear all. They would be shocked. She must submit. - -

Cartleigh leant back rakishly in his chair, and fixed his bleary, mischievous eyes .upon her. "Come, drink up, my girl; drink up 1" he hiccoughed. Her hand trembled as she raised the glass to her lips and sipped a little of the wine. "Why, you're toddy, my girl, toddy," Cartleigh said with a wicked smile, as he noted the girl's helpless plight. "S'cuse me, Ella, but we'd best be getting home." She rose to her feet unsteadily, and, as a mist rose before her eyes, grasped at her chair for support. Her partner caught her bare arm, And led her away, and when Ella woke to conscious- ness she was being whirled through the paved streets in a cab. Cartleigh was seated opposite, leaning forward with his' evil face almost touching hers, and his hands holding the opera cloak about her bare shoulders.

"Oh, you've wakened up at last, have you?" he observed, in a jeering tone; and in the dinklight of the carriage lamp Ella noted with alarm the vicious look on his drunken face.

"Are we nearly home ? " she asked, appre- hensively. s. "I dunno, my dear, I dunno. It depends on what you call home." The cab was now bowling quickly along a macadam road and ran more quickly than on the stone setts. _ _

"Where are we ? " cried Ella, peering out of the window. • _ _ _

"I dunno, I'm sure," the drunken fellow answered. "Lost my geography of this part. Don't think we are near South Africa, though, or dreary Dalesworth." He chuckled mischievously as he said this.

"We're not going home Stop, driver! Stop) r. Ella shouted, rising excitedly to her feet. SU had seen hedgerows and trees by the wayside. They were in a country lane. The road to Dalesworth was along the treeless highway. "Stop, driver! Stop ! " ,she cried, imperatively. The driver heeded not her cries. "Sit down, my dear, sit down," Cartleigh said, roughly pushing her back on to the seat. "You're all right if you'll keep quiet." The girl struggled desperately to free herself. "Now be quiet. You're all right with me, my dear, aren't you ? " He caught her round the waist and, holding her firmly in the corner of the carriage, kissed her passionately. A loud shriek, shrill and penetrating, caused thp,3 driver to stop his vehicle suddenly. "Drive on, you fool 1" roared Cartleigh, as ths man opened the door. "Not with that row going on, guv'nor," thl man growled, as he stared at the struggling girl. "Help me, oh, help me !" cried Ella beseechingly. - _ _

tt up ana arive on, you idiot!" said Cart; leigh. "Do you want to get us both into trouble ? Hearing voices up the lane and fearing that if they were discovered the consequences might be serious to himself, the driver mounted the box aud drove quickly onward.

"Now be quiet, will you?" said the profligatø. releasing the girl unwillingly. "If you object to me kissing you, it's all right. I'm not going tg hurt you, not likely. Drive round by Adderton, he shouted to the driver. "We're going home nOirs Miss Rayburn. Will that suit you ? " The distracted girl was sobbing piteously. "Come, there's no need for snivelling. There'll nothing wrong done. I thought we'd have a roundabout drive to give you time to sober IoP before you got home, that was all. You'd had a drop too much, you know; so, perhaps, had I. Come, now, dry up and let's be friends." a . But Ell?, refused to be pacified. For the rest or the journey she remained obdurate to her cow" panion's advances and when her home was reached she left him without even a parting word.

SHE FOUND OUT THE SECRET.

"Doctor," said a London lady to a famous physician. "can you tell me what causes these headaches of mine? They have been sending me to bed for two days a week for the last year and a half, and I can't stand it non& longer. 01), yea; I have taken everything—narcotics. hypnotics, nerve-foods, stimulants, and so on, until I so sick and disgusted ..with the lot of thcm. Ther stupefy me, but they don't stop the headaches, which pursue me like an enemy with murder in his heart. When the pain is gone at last, I am too tired and weak to do a thing, and a day or so later lam sure to halin another attack. Now if you can cure this trouble, so it will stay cured, you will earn every penny of your fee, whatever it is, and my lifelong gratitude besides." In his slow, gentle voice the great medical man asked his patient many qeestions—short and simple the were, and easy to answer—and then laid his soft white finger tips on the artery at her left wrist : but not Li word of comment did 'Lie utter.

" Man," he said finally," I am going to do solve th)ng unprofessional ; I am gOng to ask you to use $ medicine not compoendod or suggested by me or by an! other doctor. I do this because of the singular and won- derful way in which I first came to know of I hand it to you in a plain emulated bottle. The directions I have copied frarn the printed label and pasted theta on. The coo: etituents of this preparation I know only in part, Ye' I may confidently assure you it is harmless. You see I am giving you a very large bottle. Try it for two months and then call or write me. No, no ; thanks, but I'll accept no money now. Write, as I have requested. Good day, good day."

, Two months later the great doctor hoard from big patient. " had a vestige of Imdache for weeks," she said, " and believe I am permanently rid of it. 1 have accidentally found out what you gave moo but will never mention it in assopiation w th your nanae• I enclose note for £lOO. If you cannot conscientiously keep the money please turn it into any charity. I shall refuse to accept it back." The above is the record of an actual eocurrenee, and the names of the parties to it are in our possession. We have no authority to make them public, but there is vo reason why we should not say that the triumphant remedy was Mother Seigel's Syrup.

Any of fifty things may produce headache, but the chief cause—as in the above case—is indigestion, cot' blued with liver and kidney complaints.

EASTER CUSTOMS AND SUPERSTITIONS.

"Easter Customs and Superstitions at Home and Abroad "forms toe subject of a special article ja "The Quiver" for April, though unfortunately tlae writer has to record that most of the quaint 01a English customs are fast dying out. With the spread of education and of opportunities for travel. says the writer, popular customs in England have but a small chance of survival. Yet it is almost ion possible to say with any reasonable chance 01 accuracy if one has or has not died completely out from amongst us. In some little dip in the hills, i 0 some neglected village in the plains, it may survile in a little altered form. Take,forinstaneo,the practice of" lifting "on Easter Monday and Tuesday, which was formerly well known in the North of England. It was performed by two lusty men joining hand' ' across each other's wrists, and then having mede the person to be heaved up sit upon their arms,thei lifted him up three times into the air. This custoel had, undoubtedly, originally been intended signify the joy of the people at the Resurrection 0 our Lord. Again, the playing of football on Easter Monday was a recognised sport. The game played required no practice or pnorious knowledge of the rules. It was usually somewhat of the nature of free but friendly fight between two villages,and the Igame was ended by one party carrying the ball 1)4 sheer force to the goal of their opponents, which might be separated from their own by a mile 0! more. But football is no longer such a rough-an ready pastime, nor is it confined to one day in the year 1 "The Sussex Daily News" of June jOthf 1895, published details of a curisus Easter custoes, that still existed in Caistor Church. The vicar lieh' certain glebe on condition that if the young Me° of the parish could catch a hare on Easter Mondel and bring it to him before ten of the clock, he vrift bound, in his turn, to present them with a calr'' head and 100 eggs for breakfast, and fourpence i° money. The motive that prompted so extreerdie; ary a bequest it is difficult to imagine. At preset% it would seem that it put a premium upon the gen.lo art of the poacher. It is Baid that in some per'„ of this country there still exists the strange sup.% stition that bread baked on Good Friday, and care.; fully preserved by a family, may be used wheP crumbled into water as a remedy for many ailmento' The day of the "hot cross bun" has, however' nearly departed. A couple of generations ago there was scarce a village of any size in England, where on a Gcod Friday, the cry would not have bee° raised of:

"One a penny, two a penny, Iwo a penny buns, One a penny, twe a penny • Hot cross buns." But at present the words of the old-time dogg-rei . verse are almost forgotten, or so it would see% certain is it that the nursery knows it no longer 8 one of its regular ditties.

THE DREAM OF A WARRING-

TON lAN."

[By Arthur Bennett.l Councillor Bennett has been dreaming again, and unlike most dreamers he has been sole to fix his dream and relate the whole of it, substance and detail, under the above title. As a dreamer Mr. Bennett possesses some local notoriety, and as an author too, he is not without tame in his native town. W hetherit is.as an author or dreamer we are to treat him in this latest production of his is a question which puzzles us not a little—so much so in fact that we have dech?led to ignore the point and deal solely with what we read in the 250 pages he has writteu. In a brief preface Mr Bennett says "the history of Warrington has never yet been fully and consecutively told Yet it positively teems with interest., and through it move, in shauowy succession, many of the great- est figures of the past. To weave the broken inc'- dents into one continuous story; to seek to make 'the dry bones live';and to endeavour to bring home to its increasing multitude of citizens some real sense of all they owe to the old town of love and service is the object of the present volume." The dream commences in this way. The author wanders out to Wilderspool with our local aschae- ologist, Mr. Thomas May, and together they speculate among the remains of the old Roman Camp lying between Greenall's Avenue and the Ship Canal. They talk together of the dim historic past, and as they talk Mr. Bennett rather inconsiderately relapses tnto unconsciousnese, for "the Brewery became enveloped in a silver haze, the houses close at hand in slew succession faded from my view I lost all traces of the great canal;; and over the good old town' I knew and loved so well there seemed to creep an ever darken- ing canopy which concealed it from my sight." Mr. Bennett's dream had commenced. Lie sees the country side as it was nearly eighteen-hundred years ago when the Romans ruled the land. Tbe old camp takes form again, its garrison springs into life, and Mr. May gives place to a Roman general with whom the dreamer has an interesting conver- sation anent Boadicea, Agricola, and other worthies of this bye-gone age. Again the scene changes and we are transmitted in thought to a Norman etronghold in the neighbourhood of Howley, and so on through the cycle of centuries the author dreams his way, seeing Warrington grow from a hamlet to the town of importance it is now. The battles of the Cromwellian period, and other con- flicts, are described with the reality and pic- turesqueness of a correspondent at the front —fact, of course, being strictly adhered to. "I watched the Duke of Monmouth riding over Stoaken Heath (1682) where many of the good folk of the neighbourhood had flocked to meet him ; and later still I gazed with awe upon the Black Judge Jeffreys (whose mother was,l found, a sister of tbe second Sir Thomas Ireland) as be held his court at Bewsey Hall I stood at Market Gate and watched Lord Derwentwater, after the defeat of Preston (1715) ride, a prisoner,through the town on a white horse; . . •. . . and ere the curtain -finally descended on the tragic drama of the civil war, I saw two hundred )ebels who had come to re- connoitre, and contrived to cross the ford, surprised and captured by the gallant " Blues" from Liver- pool, who crowned their services by pulling down the central arches of the Bri go to bar 'Prince Charli6V progress." During Warrington's period of fame as "The Athens ot England" Mr. Bennett sees the Academy in its prime and gazes with aweand ad- miration on Dr. Taylor, John Bolt, Dr. Aikin, Mrs. Barbauld, Dr. Priestley, William Eyres, and other famous men and women who were connected with Warrington's literary and scientific past. And thus On to Warrington of to-day—" the town of many industries." A more interesting chronicle of the town's history could not be imagined. Mr. Bennett has taken great pains to be correct in all he says and sees,and from a number of sources be has contrived to present an excellent picture of the town as it ap- peared in each succeeding century ; 9f the changes that took place; of the buildings which disap- peared ; of the people who lived and had their be- ing:; of the events that occurred to build up the colossal fabric of history. From the past, the dreamer steps boldly into the future where he sees "The New Warrington." of 1950. In place of the old Roman Camp—where he stands once again—is a public pleasure ground,with appropriate halls and kiosks containing maps.plans and pictures which give the visitor an idea of life in the far distant period when the camp existed. The Brewery is conducted by the Corporation and so are the public-houses. Overarching limes span the Causeway and there is a majestic bridge. Beyond it in a leafy semicircle reaching from the riversdeviation to the weir, a broad em- bankment, fringed by stately buildings, and pro- vided here and there with seats. Electric traction everwhere, trains flitting by at an enormous speed tramcars traversing the streets and bound for such, gardens of Eden as Victoria Park. In his description of the latter the dreamer's imagination has full play, and between terraces, fountains, bands, kiosks, and art galleries the reader becomes almost intoxicated with delightful anticipation. There is a suspension bridge at Howley,and in defiance of engineers of to- day the weircalmly generates electricity, in which laudable task it is assisted by the tides and winds. The river is once more pellucid, fish leap from its waters, which have now a gentle mur- mur in addition. Smoke has been abolished, slums have vanished, and in their place are comforable and artistic houses for the masses. Open spaces are plentiful. and altogether the town has five beautiful parks. Howley is one huge dock. connected with an even huger dock at Arpley, both accommodating vessels of the largest size—and driven too by electricity. Overhead like mighty birds float wrist craft crowded with pas- sengers. These are a few of the wonderful improve- ments effected in Warrington during the next 50 years. Even in a dream all this cannot be accom- plished without an increase in rates but Mr. 'Sennett gets over the difficulty by attributing to the people of the next century a willingness to pay more in the for additional benefits. Evidently the ratepayer undergoes a-s great a transformation as the town itself. From material improvements turn to find that the Nonconformist Churches had federated—on a mutually satisfactory basis—with the Mother Church, and "all then were one in the love of God and of their fellow-men." Finally the dream ends, and the dreamer reflects that "some- times one's dreams come true." The book is creditably printed and contains numerous illustrations, those in the historical por- tion being excellent copies of old drawings and prints. The pages which contain Mr. Bennett's flights of fancy in the future, are embellished by drawings sprepared by Mr. J. A. Kendrick who has depicted the author's mental pictures with skill and ingenuity. Taken altogether "The dream of a Warringtenian " will be found decidedly interest- ing by inhabitants of the "good old town," who are indebted to Mr. Bennett for the publication of what is—dreams apart—the best history of local events extant. The price of the book is 2s 61.

DARESBURY SCHOOL BOARD.

The Education of Deaf and Dumb Children

INTHRESTINO POINT.

A meeting of the Darosbury School,-Roard was held on Thursday last week at Daresbury. The Rev H T Clarke presided, and there were also present Mesirs Phillips, Cooper, Warhnrtoo, Tickle, Weir, Billington, and C E Lisaker (clerk). Mr. Weir rose to move a resolution, and said:it was unfortunate that he had to do it himself. as it had reference to his own child. He moved that the Board pay £lO 68i, per annum for the educatio a of his child at Old Trafford Deaf and Dam h Institution, Manchester. That,, he believed, was the amount tin Board were legally bound to pay. Be did nor., of course, ask any- thing for thu child'i maintenaace or clothing. He paid for those himself. Mr. Phillips : Is this for the second by or the one already at tho institution Mr. Weir said he asked the grant for h;s second son, who was at present at home. Towards the education of the boy at the institution the Board paid £8 64 Bd. per annum, and he had to pay the rest of the necessary sum for his education, as well as having to pay for his m tin- tenance and clothing. Education was free now-a-days, and he conteuded that he should net have to pay any- thing for the education of his children. If they were not afflicted they could be educated at the Board Schools free of charge. The Clerk said the Board were under no obligation to costribute to the cost of educating either of the children, but they could grant any sum if they chose up to a certain limit, which was prescribed in the code of the education department. Mr. Weir : Wray should I pay for education? There is free education everywhere else. Mr. Billington then seconded Mr. Weir's resolution. Mr. Weir remarked that he was paying £2 per annum now which the Board ought to be paying for the other bay. Mr. Warburton sail that if Mr, Weir was not in a position to pay, the Board might be obliged to contri- bute the whole sum. In further discussion it was stated that the full amount charged by the institution was £l7 6s ed per annum for each child, Mr. Cooper said he thought the matter should be fully enquired into. Mr. Weir wanted the Board to contribute , £lO 163 83., and he himself would have to pay about £7 which was only 3s a week. He contended emphatically ' that Mr. Weir could not keep his boy for such a small sum at home. It was too little for him to expect to pay. Mr. Weir pointed out that be was asking for money for the education of his zhild,not for its maintenance. Mr. Cooper then moved an amendment that the Clerk be instructed to write to the Education Department asking what amount if any the Board was bound to pay for the education and maintenance of the child at Old Trafford. -

Mr. Phillips secondol. Mr. Linaker : I think you will have a reply that there i; some discretionary pQwer in the hands of the Board. .If Mr. Weir's contention is correct, a porson exceedingly well oft might come to the Board ad say "I have a deaf and dumb child, and you will have to pay for its education." Mr Linaker then read a note from the education code to the effect that if a school authority incurecl any expense in respect of a child, the parent was liable to contribute toward the expense such weekly sum as might be agreed upon by the schocl authority. Mr. Cooper's amendment was agreedito.

Austin and Patrick Maginn at 5-30.—Mr. Arthur Browne appeared on behalf of the prosecutors, and the defendant was represented by Mr. W. El J. SOMETKiAG ABOUT TROOP- HORF,ES.

Ie "Cassell's Migazinel" for April, M. Fletcher Robin- I son tells of e. chat with a sergeant in the Teeth, (Prince of Wales's Own Royal) Hussars :—The Tenth were quarted at Canterbury when I was introduced to Try friend the sergeant. The two facts that I belonged to •his county and was a profound admirer of his regiment predisposed him towards me. When he discovered that I was also something of a connoisseur in horse flesh his ' la•,t trace of reserve vanished. "A good horse, sir, Is like a good wife," said the sergeant sententiously : "they're both precimus hard to find. Sense! Why, there's horses in my squadron as Feasible as you and me." I told the sergeant I could quite believe it. "In course, regiments are restricted in the matter of colour," he continued ; "but give me a free hand, and I should pick a roan—that is, for good temper and quick learning. Dark greys and blacks are mostly strong and hardy, and, so are dark chestnuts. La a general mire, light cheetnuta and light bays are nervous and delicate. A rusty black es a sulky pig nine times out of ten. Tnen, again, there are 'white stock- line,' as they call it. You know the old saying, 'One white leg's a bad 'an, two white legs you may sell to a friend, three white legs yen may triest for a time, four white legs you may lay your life on.' " "Horses take curious dislikes sometimes." said I. "That'a true, both to men and to each other. The Tenth occe had a brute that was nearly cast for temper. He'd hardly let a man come into hs stall to feed him, and as for greoining—well, it was nigh impossible. There happened at the time to be a men in the regiment who was as wild as they make 'em, always before the colonel for some font's trick or other. Well, he was given the horse, and they took to each other at once. One day he went reeling across into the stable and fell by the side of his horse in a drunken sleep; but when the-guard came to take him to clink, bless you, they couldn't get near him. His horse stood over him with his ears back, and sort of dared them to come on ! Then, again, horses will get some bad bleed between these and fight when- ever they get a chance. They tell a story of two arab chargers we once bad which got such a hatred of each other that they were always separated. One night on a troopship one or them broke loose and jumped clean over the beck of the steady old chap next to him te get at the other."

THE PIG ON A CHURCH STEEPLE.

The Legends About Winwick Church..

One of the mist curious legenle connected with a church (and which of our old churches have not a legend ?) is the one about the pig on Winwick Church steeple. This church, dedicated to St. Oswald, stands on an eminence, commanding an extensive view, about three miles north of Warrington. The livine of Win- wick is said to be the richest in Eegland ; it was for- merly valued at £3,616 per annumaand is the gift of the Earl of Derby. The Rev. J. J. Hornby, one of the rec- tors. spent £3,400 in erecting a handsome chapel of ease at Croft in his own parish,and contributed £6,000 to the building of a church and school in the township of Dewnall Green, besides a further sum of £l,OOO towards the cost of a church at Newton. 13; his benevolence no less than five daughter churches were added to theparent one,though the income of the rector was reduced thereby. The benefice is still worth about £2 per year ner hsal of the population, which, being rural, is under 900. Winwick is supp'sed on good authority to be the ancient British Carer Guintuic, and to have been the seat of one of the twelve Saxon chiefs who formed their establhhments in South Lancashire 'before the institution of parishrs. It seems to have been the favourite resi- dence of Oswald, king of Northumbria ; he is said to have fallen in battle here, Winwick being in the district of Makerfield (battlefield). Some years after hie death the good folks of Winwick decided to build a church te the memory of St. Oswald, a well, bearing his name, being supposed to work miraculous cures. Accordingly they cleared a apace in the valley, laid the foundations, and began to build the walle.but in the night the ir,hatetants heard a weird cry of Wee-ee-wick, Wee-te-wick, which continued the whole night. In the meruing every stone of the church had been removed to the top of the hill, a pig being discovered rolling up the last stone with its snout, crying Wee-se-wick. Its task accomplished, the pig vanished into the earth, so the people considered that it was a spirit in the form of a pig sent to teach them that their church must he built on the hill, over the very spot where King Oswald died. The village was ever afterwards known by the name of Winwick, and a stone pig was placcl half-way up the church steeple. Some ate' the pie, was placed there as part of the arms of Oswald, and yet another legend states that a pig possessed with an .evil spirit prowled over the neighbourhood. inflicting irriory on man arid beast, but was at length exorcised, the pig taking refuge up the church tower, where it was turned to stone. Anyhow the pig remains on the steeple to this dev, though scarcely visibns to the naked eye. The church is large and irreeular, of carious a:;ei, and evidently existed before the Conquest. The present tower was built la 1350. The interior of the church is interesting, not only from the ancieat trussed roof adorned with the Stanley arms, the clustered columns with the Tudor flower, and a noble organ, the gift of the late rector, but also from the numerous sepulchral honours of the Gerards, Leighs, Hornbys, and Stanley's. In the Civi War. Cromwell is slid to have slain a thousand men and taken two thousand prisoners who took refuge in the church. A new rectory was built at Win wick in 1887 to com- memorate her Majesty's Jubilee. The Old Ball, which formerly served as a rectory, is now used for an asylum. It is a substantial building standing in a well-wooded park.—" New Penny Magazine."

In answer to questions by Mr Johnson, who represented the Official Receiver, the bankrupt stated that he com- menced business with £2OO capital. In ' . arriving at his contracts he gauged the amount by: that which was paid for a similar class of hous.. Beyond that his contracts were pure guesswork. Ile could not say how much per cent the materials had increased in price but he knew that bricks were five shillings a thou- sand dearer. Ile admitted that he had engsged in rash and 1-111rdous building speculations. lEe Pd not, kept proper books of accounts. ;-,•(:(1. furnAuve in June he was not aware that he wee insolvent—The examination was closed.

THE EQUIPMENT OF WAR CORRESPONDENTS.

The equipment of a war correspondent," says Robert Maeuray, in his article on .` The Soldiers of the Frees," which appears in the April number of the " Windsor Ma.gezine." " depends a good deal on the nature of the country where the fighting is going on, whether the climate is hot or cold, and BO forth. Ia the Busso-Turkish war each correspondent had a wagon,' writes Mr. Forbes, ' a pair of draught horses, saddle horses, a (soupier of servants, and couriers at discretion.' The late G. W. Steevens, in one of his books, tells us that in the Greco-Turkish war he had • one dragoman, one caves, two saddle horses, two peck horses, saddle and bridle Engl:sh style, saddle and bridle 'Turkish style, two pack-saddles, brushes and currycomb, halters, nose-bags, rope, two kit-bags, a chair, a table,. a fez, a waterproof sheet, towels, knives, forks, spoons, a few yards of waterproof canvas, a , bed, a pillow, a quilt, a cartridge-b&t, water-bottle, bucket, quinine, hypermangauate of potassium, frying- pan, teapot, japanned dishee and plates, cups and mugs, two lanterns, a cheap watch, a thousand cigarettes, champagne, whisky and other liquids, hams and tongues, tea, sugar, cocoa, tinned beef, salmon and herrings, sardines, salt, biscuits, Worcester sance,cheese, Ea.o's Fruit Salt, corned beef,' and a great many other things besides—a sort of miniature edition, in fact, of the Army and Navy Stores. It is a truly appalling catalogue, but Mr. Steevens at the time was in Turkey, where the commissariat was of the most primitive character. The bieycle of the correspondent was first used in this war by Frederick Villiers and Wilfred Pollock. Mr. Charles Williams, vrho has been a correspondent in eight campaigns, told use that he generally had with hire three h0r593 or three camels (the latter in the Soudan) for riding or for carrying baggage, as the case might be, and always two or three servants. But, indeed, every newspaper man in the field has to be similarly equipped. Be is not stinted as to means ny his people at home, and is practically given a free hand to presure whatever he thinks desirable for the accomplishment of the ends in view. It used to be I alleged that a certain great journal gave carte blanche to its representatives to order special trains or at Bafflers ad if by doing so the transmission of important news would be facilitated. The statement may not be exactly true, as special trains and steamers are very ex- pensive luxuries ; but it is quite conceivable that emergencies mignt arise in which a war correspondent would be justified in spending large sums of money in procuring intelligeece. Steamers were hired by news- papers in the Cuban war, and were made free We of by American j mrnalists. But, as a rule, the heaviest ex- penditure is connected with the telegraphing of the de:.;- patches themselves, though, of course, this depends on the distance. The telegraph bill of our great daily newspapers reaches largo figures even in times of peace, but during a war their cutgoings under this head must be simply enormous. The expenses incurred by the war cerreT)ndents and their salaries can be but mere trifles when compared with the cost of their telegrams."

They are built of steel franoewnrk,vchich is carried up storey after storey, and in the early stages looks more like slaffoldina than part of the permanent building. Th 9 mite.. walls are fitted in afterwards, though when the framework is completed, it k quite possible to put the roof on before these are com:nf ncQd. At present the tallest of these buildings in .I`.lcv York goes up twenty- nine storevs, and from the pavement to the top, it is nearly 400 feet high. This, however, is but a comparatively small affair to another stritc!..ure, which is projecttd, and which would be no less than 1500 feet blab, ar ball as high ao'aili as the Eiffel Tower. Among other advanta.les those on the top floors would almost enjoy the lo,urielits if mountain air, hut such buildings would scare dr recommend them- selves to nervous people who prefer to live in one or two I s',orev houses in case of fire at night.

===== 0002601_19000412_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
1111 E EVA. MINER,

APRIL 12Ti, 1900.

WITH the second reading The Session So of the Agricultural Hold- Far.

ings Bill the session has reached the first recess, and some estimate of its general results may be expedient. It is needless to say that the Government has been able to accomplish almost everything that it intended to do. it was easily able to withstand the usual motions used on the Address, and the discussions indicated the great variety of opinion which marks the present Chamber, and has been considerably increased by the war. The discipline of the Union- ist Party has been severely maintained, important dissidents from the South African policy of the Government being unable to break through the solidity and coherence of the rank and file. Sir EDWARD CLARKE has retired, and others less dis- tinguished have either been temporarily silenced or have been compelled to exchange counsels with very angry supporters. The Irish members have consistently and often noisily opposed the war and its financial arrangements,gathering up with them some help, varying very much in quantity, from the general Opposition. The Liberal section which offers uncompromising opposition to the war has not grown in size nor influence, if it has been sometimes an embarrassment to Sir H. CAMPBELL- BANNERMANin his efforts to pull the party to- gether, and to evoke a policy of criticism and patriotism at once in :Aerate and influential. There have been no bitter speeches of note upon either side, and, as a whole, the Opposition has assisted the Government in its military and financial arrangements, without winning from the Minis- terialists any duo Meed of praise for its level-headed course of action. The principal business of the session so far has been military and financial. An inquiry is promised into the administration of the War Office, and also into some contract scandals which have arisen in connection with the supply of inferior goods and the presence on the War Office , contract-list of firms which had previously been struck off. The House of Commons has assisted in , , the means devised for carrying on the war, for the I permanent increase of the army,ancl for those tem- porary measures of defence not yet carried out,and almost unnecessary now, so rapidly have the public tremours and fears subsided. The new taxes, omitting the produce-contract tax, have been sanctioned, and the war loan has been popu- lar. The legislative efforts of the Government have been nearly all introduced under the Ten Minutes Rule—an abuse which has provoked much hostile comment. Some of the bills have been read a second time, and hence public business is in a very forward state. Taking from private mem- bers much of their time, and repressing debate on the Ministerial side, the Government has been able to make great progress and to avoid discus- sions which might have been inconvenient. The new education code is to be amended to meet objections which were getting so powerful as to impel the withdrawal of the whole of the new pro- visions. There have been occasions on which Mr. BALFOUR'S leadership has hardly been a success, and he has been unable to give the House the direction which it had a right to expect. The Liberal party has been in a very difficult position, and the critics of Sir H. CAMPBELL-BANNERMAN'S leadership have not always taken into account the internal condition of the party and the danger of more robust tactics. Leading skeleton battalions into combat with a compact enemy is never a wise proceeding, and it is more than questionable that greater decision and vigour of attack would have contributed, to success. The Liberal peers have, of course, been unable to achieve anything, but they have been in harmony with the leaders in the Commons, and they have upheld a high standard of policy in all international affairs. Easter will find the Liberals in better spirits then they were when the session opened.

THE first impression re- The Indian Finance Commission. spccting the report of

the Royal Commission on Indian finance will be ono of satisfaction that no glaring defects have been discovered, and the second will probably he one of surprise that no striking remedies and reductions are suggested. It is said that the control is effective, and that no undue pressure results from the disproportionate strength of the military element in the Council. Unfortunately, there is no acceptance of the changes advised in the minority report,which would give more direct representative and actual con- trolling power to Indian members. The cost of ollecting revenue is not regarded as excessive, but the general accounts of revenue and expenditure should be made up to the end of the calendar year. The Mutiny Debt has been reduced consider- ably, but at the present rate it will require seventy years to pay it off. A famine reserve of a prmanent nature is recommended. The central questions to be considered refer to military charges, and here there are important changes advocated, though they are hardly as drastic as had been exee3ted. The loss on strate- gic railways should be transferred to military ex- penditure,in the opinif n of the majority, and as to the increase in the non-effective military charges, about which there has been so much debate in

Parliament, it is admitted that they areformi& able. Actuarial reports are suggested on this head as well as upon military pensions. Apportioning charges between India and the United Kingdom is met rather boldly. It is affirmed that £50,000 a year should be voted by Parliament towards the cost of the India Office, and half the cost of the military charges for Aden and the Persian MiE- sion, but that other arrangements should be con- tinued. It is not allowed that India suffers by the Short Service system under which some ultimate saving may be possible. Pensions are left unal- tered. A distinct uaderstanding ought to 13 arrived at between England and India as to the payment of Indian troops employed out Of India. A tribunal of arbitration has been sketched —the minority report favours the Privy Council as the proper body--but it can only be "one of medi- ation." The better plan would be to come to an understanding, and a basis is outlined for it which seems fair enough. The scope of the minute of regulation is as follows: India has a direct inter- est in the Suez Canal, in the Persian Gulf, in Afghanistan, and in Siam. She has a naodified interest in the East African coast and the African islands in the Indian Ocean, except Mada gascar. She has no "direct and substantial interest" ill Europe, in Af.ica West of the Cape of Good Hopay or in Asia east of China, or in the African coast south of Zanzibar. When the two Governments cannot agree no contribution should be made by India until ' the sanction of Parliament has been obtained. Nothing important ie advised respecting India's naval contribution— Generally speaking, the Royal Commissioners sug- gest for further guidance that there should be strict impartiality in financial arrangements, end watchfulness on the part of the House of Con.-. mons ; but that India, "as a component part GE the Empire, must be prepared to share in the cost of a system the expense of which may be enhanced for Imperial purposes." It is also thought that the United Kingdom might reasonably pay half the cost of the transport of troops to an.l from India. The actual financial relief to India that would result from these changes cannot be estimated, but it would be considerable, and perhaps amount to something under £200,000 a year. Whenever it ie proposed to make this addition to our home expen- diture, it is evident that keener interest than at present will be taken in Indian financial and mili- tary administration.

It is expected that during the coming summer the number of English tourists in Ireland will top the record. Whatever may be said to the con,. trary, the feeling excited by the war, which has been so manifest in the foreign Press, wili cause a decline this year in Continental touring, and even the attractions of the Exhibition, will fail to induce Britishers in any very great number to stay in Paris durng the four sweltering weeks in August which is the time when most people take their holidays. There will be many trips for a feW days across the Channel, and at Whitsuntide the gay city will be full, but later the visitors will be recruited more from America than from our own country. It is of course preposterous for anyone' to think that the French or any other nation would treat with discourtesy English travellers coming to spend money amoog them. But there' will be evervwhero, except in Italy,an undisguised criticism of British policy and British warfare in South Africa; and as no one can tell how long this war will last, and holiday arrangements are gener- ally made early,the inclination will be to avoid the chance of these comments. Political differences,of course, are still acute in Ireland, but the English tourists who go there and give and receive liespi• tality will do a good deal to soften them,following in remoter districts the example set by the Queen in Dublin.

Professor Huxley's ohservation about dirt being "matter in the wrong place" is being remembered at the present time, when refuse is being re- garded as a valuable asset. The dream of several corporations has been to utilise their collections of waste from the streets and houses as fuel for running dynamos to supply electric light. That has been realised in several towns, and the ashesr , dry vegetable stalks, and such things are in those places no longer thrown about the building grounds to make doubtful foundations where a dip occurs. But it appears that an ingenious persotle inspired,no doubt,by the action of the corporations has patented a plan to turn the ash dust and floor sweepings into easily portable fuel. His belief is, that it may be made, so far as it will go, a substi- tute for coal. He is anticipating that time tO which Mr. Courtney looks forward so gloomilYr when eur coal mines will b 3 exhausted, or near exhaustion, and our industries thereby paralysed. By the employment of neglected materials for heat-giving and by the increased use of electriciti for motor purposes this evil may be postponed.M a matter of fact, however, the diminution of cot is not likely to be seriously felt until another generation is well advanced ii. life, and so in anY action we take in this matter we are very kind to posterity.

There are bounds to even a footballer's libertf of action as several who were called upon to appeet at the Warrington Borough Police Court on Mon' day now know to their cost. They were rnemberg of the Seacon:ibe Swifts Football Club, but the height and front of their &lone° did not lie iø their swiftness, hut rather in their rowdyiere' Between the two there is, of course, a wide differ" once, and anyone inclined to reckon it from monetary standpoint would say, judging from the experience of the Seacombe men, that the differ; once is at least ten shillings. These ger,tlernen the jersey and knickerbockers from their Merse9; swept shore seem to have been overjoye" at the result of their encounter with the Warrington Association Club, and endeavoured to let the people of Warrington know of it during their passage through the quiet and orderlY thoroughfares of the "good old town "so notice- able on a Saturday night.
