RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

SUCCESS OF MR. G. Le.ws.

On Saturday, Mr. George Laws, son of Mr. John Laws, of Widnes, was successful, out of 122 candi- dates, for the post of assiatant sanitary inspector for Eccles, near Manchester. The honour was alto- gether unexpected, and the news caused much pleasure amongst his friends. He is only nineteen years of age. MERSIIY MISSION TO Swi. The weekly concert was given in th„ Seamen's Institute by the young people connected With the mis4on on Friday evening The programme was a repatition of the concert which was given a week previously, and was much enjoyed by a large num- ber of sailors and friends. The concert was the last of the season. and Mr. Shaw, during a short speech, said he was pleased to see that the entertainments had all been so well attended.

---..- STREET ACCIDENT. -A. CONTRACTOR THROWN OUT

OF HIS TRAP,

About quarter past ten, on Wednesday morning, as Mr W a Moores, contractor, Runcorn, was pro- ceeding up High-street, in his trap, from some un- accountable reason the horss walked over a box be- fore a tradesman's shopmear to Ilazlehurst's Soapery with the result that Mr Moores was thrown out of the trap, under the horse's feet. Luckily,the horse was brought under control,aud Mr Moores extricated from his predicament, escaping with but slight bruises.

===== 0002601_19000412_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
WANTED AT PAIL.

At the Runcorn- Police Court, on Friday, before Mr J Ockleston, James Armstrong, sailor, was charged with assaulting a woman at Par, Cornwall, on the 2lth March. The prisoner was apprehended on board the steamer Norman, at Runcorn Docks, on Wednesday evening, by Sergeant Gregory and P.C. Bowyer. The magistrates remanded the prisoner until the arrival of an escort. .fle was sub- sequently handed over to Inspector Kittow of the Cornwall police. DA.IIA.GE TO RUNCORN BOUND VESSELS. On Friday evening last,the Ship Canal Company's Hopper, No. 1, was towing a couple of vessels to Runcorn. As they entered the Bastham Lock, the foremost vessel collided violently with the lock wall, her bowsprit being carried away and her bows considerably damaged. With being brought up so suddenly, the second vessel also collided with her stern, and caused further damage to the bulwarks on that quarter. The vessels which were the "Selina" and "David Rees" were eventually towed to Runcorn without further mishap. The damage to the "Selina" is estimated at about £lOO.

DEATH OF AN OLD INHABITANT. Intelligence reached Runcorn on Monday, of the death of Mrs. Jane Hardcastle, relict of the late Mr. William Hardcastle, of 10, Waterloo-road Mrs. Hardcastle left Runcorn some few years ego to go and reside with her son at the Endowed Schools, Sawbridgewerth, Herts. She had but a short illness. Taking ill on the previous Monday she rallied a little on Tuesday and Wednesday. On Thursday evening pleurisy set in,and despite all that medical aid could do for her she succumbed on the follow- ing Saturday night. The news has come as a great shock to her Runcorn friends. The funeral took place on Tuesday last. THE SOUTH AFRICAN WAR AT THE PUBLIC HALL. We would call our readers' attention to the fact that Mr. A. Cheetham, of Rhyl, pays a visit to the Public Hall with the " Silvograpn." The instru- ment is a vary remarkable one, and the "living pic- tures " thrown on the screen by it are life-like and perfect. The subject to be dealt with is the Trans- vaal War, and new film+ direct from the seat of war will be exhibited, including the Modder River Battle. Lancers fording the river, General Buller embarking, the 47 gun in action, charge of the 12th Lancers, the capture of a maxim gun, and many others, Mr. W Roselle will also appear in " Flashes of Fan," and Madame Rose Garton, a vocalist of no mean power, will also contribute to the evening's entertainment. FIRE AT RUNCORN LAY-BY.

About 630 7n—Saturday evening a fire' broke out in the hold of the sailing vessel, Castle Reck, lying in the Ship Canal at Runcorn Lay-by. It seems that the vessel arrived at the Docks on Friday,from San Francisco, with a large cargo of wheat. About 6.30 p.m., on Saturday there was a considerable number of the crew on deck, and one of the men accident- ally threw a lighted match into the held. This ignited a bag ot wheat, and the flames were spread- ing when the crew, finding what had occurred, promptly smothered the fire. Three bags of wheat were destroyed their value being 7e each. The cargo was insured. CANON WOOD AND IRREVERENCE IN CHURCII. In the current number of the Parish Magazine" the Vicar makes the following pertinent ratnarks "Reports are rife of irreverent behaviour on the nart of grown girls on Sunday evenings at the Parish Church. I trust that members of the con- gregation will have the moral courage to interfere or call the attention of the church officers; and it is well that such offenders should know,if their own sense of right and wrong is asleep, that the law is severe against disturbers of divine worship, and will, it necessary, be put in force. I shall be grate- ful to anyone wko will send me the name of an offender, in order that if words of kindness will not serve, other means may be tried for the protection of the worshippers." A Bo Y DECAPITATED AT WIDNEB.

On Sunday night a terrible fatality occurred at Widnes. A boy named William Robert Smith. aged four years, a son of Thomas Henry Smith, chemical labourer, residing at 15, Warrington-road, Widnes, went to play on some waste land at the front of his home. He was seen to go to the railway siding be- longing to the London and North-Western Railway Company, which is about 300 yards distant from his father's house at the rear of Dennis's copperworks with a number of other boys. There were on the siding four waggons, which were stationary, and de- ceased was observed to be running round the wag- gons. Someone touched the brakes, and the trut:lcs began to rush down the incline. At the time the deceased was under the waggons and someone shouted to him to lie down. A. b3V named Forster saw the deceased lying on the rails between two waggon wheels. His head was cut off. The other bovi ran away, and Forster told a man named Bos • cow what had happened. Boscow went to the sid- ing and found the deceased with his head in the four-feet. He pulled the body away and discovered that the head was almost severed from the body, it only hanging by a piece of skin. He removed the deceased to his late home.

T-11- It- LATE COLOUR-SERGEANT JOHN WHITEHEAD.

The following reference ta Colour-Sergeant J. Whitehead, of the 2nd Battalion Royal Lancashire Regiment, who lied of wounds received in action at Railway Hill, near Colenso, on the 27th February, appears in the current issue of the •' Runcorn Parish Ntegazine :—" He was an old scholar of the Parish Church Day and Sunday Schools,and many of those now connected with our church, and who remember Jack Whitehead as a lad at school and had a strong affection for him, received the news of his death with much sorrow. He was every inch a soldier, brave, strong, self-reliant; and his manner of life told that he had not only learned the soldier's lesson of strict discipline, but had applied it to his own charactei. He wrote to a Runcorn friend shortly after the battle of Scion Kop, in which he was engaged, full of hope as to the issue of the efforts being made for the relief of those in Ladysmith,and only a few hours before their realization Jack died the soldier's death. Hoch sympathy is felt for his brother William (also an old scholar of ours), who was fighting almost side by side with him, and for others of his relatives and friends,"

RUNCORN iIARRITIRS.

Saturday last Saw the close of the cross-country season in this district. The Runcorn Harriers finished with aSi miles road handicap from the Bull's Head. Out OF an entry of 27, 14 started. The route slightly exceeded the distance for which the handicap was framed, but the race nevertheless was an interesting one, and reflects great credit on the handicappers. The route selected was to the RAd Lion at Moore and back again. The starts conceded by the scratch man were upwards of eight minutes. As was generally expected, Thomas took up the running on the outward jaurney with Hookins,RaV and Goodall closely follevving, the pace being a hot one throughout. The finish was not over exciting, Ray winning by 159 yards, having covered the dis- tance in 52 min.. off 10 sees. Thomas wes second. having covered the distance in 52 mins , 50 sec., off scratch : Goodall third, finishing the distance in 53 mini., 55 sees. off 15 secs. Hopkins won the conso- lation prize, off 20 secs. S Shallcroes won the novice medal, off six minutes The handicap throughout was a success, and reflects great credit on Ur '2 Richardson, who had the working of the com- petition. and who has done so much during the V4S; season to bring this claes of sport forward. The first prize was given by Mr Luxton, the second prize by Mr Hough. and the ether prizes by sulwritetions from,oe following q.ntlemen:—J Levatt..T Dutton, 1' Richardson, H Friend, F Lamb, it Ray, T Faulkner, E Broder, A Gee, W Knight, J Kogerson, and others.

Defendants who were given an alternative of seven days paid I the money.

DEATH OF MR. JAMES 1.4/PDY. The older tradespeople of Runcorn who remem- bered Mr. James Liddy, when he kept a musical store in High-street, Runcorn, will hear with regret of his death which took place at Longsight, Man- chester, on Friday night last, at the advanced age of 76 years. For many years past Mr. Liddy has been living in Manchester, and carrying on his pro- fession as piano tuner, &c.,; prior to that he carried on business in Runcorn and Southport as an exten- sive musical dealer.—The funeral took place on Monday last. RINCORN WESLEYAN FORRIGN MISSIONS. An interim meeting was held in Brunswick Sun- day School, on Sunday afternoon last, presided over by one of the teachers.(who fulfil the duty in rotation), Mr John H Darlington. There was a very good attennance, some members of St. Paul's con- gregation being present. Twe excellent addresses were delivered by Mr J Hayes and the Rev J A B Malvern.The report read by one of the school seen- taries,Mr Unsworth,showed that during the past Ave months £32 153 41 had been raised, made up as follows :—Collected in classes, £lO 6361 ; collected by scholars with books £l9 2s 2d •, collected at the meeting, .£3 53 8d; total £32 153 4d. RUNCORN SCHOOL BOARD. The monthly meeting of the Board was held on Thursday evening last week, the Rev. Canon Mait- land Wood (chairman) presiding. There were also present :—Rev J Chambers, Messrs. E Imison, F J Norman, W J Cunningham,J Pritchard,W Handley, J Littler, J IV Lightburn (clerk), and A L'ghtburn (assistant clerk).—The estimates of expenditure for the half year ending September were submitted, and it' was decided to issue a precept on the over- seers for £l,OOO to meet the same.—The Clerk sub- mitted the financial statement for the halt year end- ing March 25th, and the same was approved.—The Clerk read a letter from the Association of School Boards, forwarding a memorandum with reference to the new Code, and requesting that the Board should immediately use its influence with the mem- bers of Parliament in their locality to secure the postponement of the new regulations for at least twelve moeths. During some discussion the Chair- man (Canon Wood) pointed out that Mr. Balfour had promised that a memorandum should be issued and that the Code would be discussed in Parliament. It was ultimately decided, • on the motion of Mr Handley, seconded by Mr. Norman, that no action be taken until the promised memorandum was issued, when a special meeting of the Board would be called.

Mr. Woodward said he quite agreed with Mr. Linaker and perhaps the standing order had been transgressed. He, however, was not pres3nt at that meeting. It was a nice distinction.

Mr. Stokes: That being so the resolution would not stand.

• The Clerk said be was of opinion everything was in order. At the previous meeting the committee was appointed to consider the surveyors' salaries, and the question of a steam roller, and to report upon both at the next =citing. It also appeared on the agendas sent round that the committee would repert upon those sub- jeeZa to the Cenncil.Of course,it might have been lx.tter, nad there been a fuller setting out of the facts, and if the Council thought proper he would take it as an in- struction to issue fuller details upon the next occasion. He did net, for a moment, hold the opinion that the resolutions were void. After some further discussion the matter was left on the above understanding.

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The mull fortnightly meeting of the above Board was held at the Workhouse, Dutton, on Tuesday. Present : Messrs. J Moseley Williams, (chairman) ; S Taylor, J Meadowcroft, T Reynolds. Dr. Gastaldi, with the Rural District Councillors, Mr. George F Ashton (clerk), Mr W E Hough (assistant clerk), and LMr. Joseph Cunnah (master). "BEING CP THE CHILD IN THE WAY IT SHOULD GO." The Clerk read a circular from the Board of Education calling the attention of managers of elementary scheols situate in agricultural districts to the importance of making the e location in the village school more con- sonant with the environment of the schalars than was usually the case.—The natural activities of children should be turned to useful account—their hands should be trained to some of the practical &Verities .of rural life, and not merely to the use of pen and pencil, and that they should be taught when circumstances per- mitted Low to hamdle the simpler tools that were used on the garden or farm. The matter was submitted to the Parliamentary Com- mittee to report.

THN POOR LAW CONFERENCE.

The Chairman submitted the report of the delegates appoiated to attend the Poor Law Conference held at London,on the 29th and 30th of March last. The report was a brief summary of the work accomplished by the Conference. Having referred to the beneficial education delegate; attending the Conferences received in poor law administratien, Mr. Williams proposed that copies of the reports of the Conference be obtained and given to every member of the Board.

Mr. T. Darlington, who stated that tbe visit to the Conference had been new and instructive to him, added there was a great deal to be iearnt, and he felt that every Guardian should have a cooy of the proceedings. He did not go so far as to say they were" all-perfect " in Runcorn Workhouse, but he could say, as A constquence of what he had heard, that the Runcorn Workhouse would compare favourably with other workhouses in the country. (Hear. bear.) He bad been round the house that day, and had noticed almost all the comforts referred to in a very able paper submitted at the Conference by a lady delegate. There was, however, one thing he notized as missing. A MURDER CONFESSED AT RUNCORN, PRISONER REMANDED.

Before Mr. W TirataiaB, at; the Runcorn Police Court, on Wednesday morning, George Thornton, alias Herne,:wai charged with on the 15th of April, 18S13, then being a eubj-ct of our Lady .•:overign the Queen, on land out of the United Kingdom, he did wilfully, unlawfully and with intent kill and murder one George Gorman, alias Black.--Sergeant Gill, divisional clerk at Runcorn, swat.° that on the precious day the prisoner presented himself at the office, about 11 o'clock. He then appeared to have had some drink, and was detained. He took down. in writing the following statement made by the prisoner, who was a private in the akh Regiment. "In April, 18?6, 1 was stationed at Fozepol East Indies, when 1 quarrelled with a private in the same regiment. named Black. He called me a bastard as I came out of the canteen. We fought between the canteen and the quartermaster's stores. I knocked him down, He lay on the ground uncon- scious. Whilst he lay on the ground I robbed him of 50 roupees which I afterwards hid for a time. I spent the money afterwards. I picked him up and threw him down a well which is between the can- teen and the quartermaster's stores. His body was foetid next flay in the well and was taken out. An inquest was held and an open verdict returned This man was nicknamed Black but his proper name was George Gorman. Before signing this Paper it was read over to me in the presence of Superintendent, D Pearson, who cautioned me that it might be used:in evidence against me." Prisoner also made a subsequent statement partly exonerat- ing himself.—Supetiotsnient D Pearson asked for a remand to the 19 so that inquiries could be made.—Prisoner said he had been drinking, and as he was a great sufferer from liver complaint he said anything when in drink. Such a crime had , been committed and he was one of three suspected of it, so he might have had it on his mind.—The , Magistrate: You will be remanded for eight days.

The annual fortnightly meeting of the above Council was held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday. There were present: Messrs. J. HELSBY SEWAGE PURIFICATION WORKS. THE QUESTION OF SITE. At a meeting of the Parish Conned Committee held in the National Schools, Helsby, on Monday, the 2ad inst., Mr. James Taylor presiding, the Chi k reported that in accordance with instructions given at the last meeting he had been in communication with the owners of the tsiece of land which the Council wished to purchase for a site for sewerage purification works, and that he had received a reply from the owner that he did not wish to sell. The owner of the piece of land which the com- mittee wished to purchase for a pumping station wrote stating that he would sella piece of land at the rate of £l5O per acre. He further reported he had consulted the Surveyor as to another suitable site for a purifidation works and that the Surveyor had recommended a piece of land in Alvanley. It was resolved that the consider- ation of the question be deferred until more definite re- plies have been received. It was resolved diet the sum of £l5 be paid to the Clerk for his services to the com- mittee. These minutes were confirmed on the motion of Mr. C. E. Linaker. FRODSHAM KNOWS WHAT IT REQUIRES. The Local Government Board forwarded two copies of an order they had issued investing the Rural District Council of Runcorn with certain urban powers as regards the contributory place of Frodsham Township in their district, and declaring the expenses incurred by the Council in the exscution of those powers to be special expenses. The Clerk asked for instructions to publish the order, as according to its terms it had to be published before the 17th inst. Mr. Linaker moved that the Clerk be instructed to advertise the order in some local paper at once. Wtsilst upon his feet he also took the opportunity of pointing out that Mr. Dansey was not right in his prediction that the order had not been granted without a local inquiry being held. Also that Mr. Edwards was wrong when he had implied that the Frodsham pocomittel did not know what they wanted, for it was now clearly shown that the Frodsham Committee were understood by the Local Government Board, and that was ad they .required. The motion was seconded and unanimously agreed to. THE POLLUTION OF HATT3N BRoiK. Mr John Lyon Whittle, Town Clerk, of Warringten„ wrote enquiring what s'!eps s the Rural District Ceuncil had determined to take to put a stop to the pellutien of Hatton Brook. The Corporation had now agreed with Sir Gilbert Greenall as to the position of the proposed reservoir at tDaresbury: It occurred to him (the Town Clerk), that it would facilitate matters if the clerk and surveyor were to meet the water engineer and himself to discuss the matter. Mr. Ashton explained that as a result of that letter the eurveyor and himself and the town clerk had met and conferred on the matter. When the Warrington Corpor- ation Act of 1823 was being passed} it was asked that a clause should be inserted to protect this Council if the works of the Corporation should in any way interfere with the outfalls of the Corporation. At that time, too, the clause so far as the making of the rtiservoir near 'gotten was concerned was struck out of the Bill ; there- fore, such a clause as they found in the Bill would not in any way help the Council as against the Corporation. Now he understood the Corporation weremaking arrange- ment with the landlord for the construction of the , reservoir. At the interview with Mr. Whittle the point was mentioned to him and he said so far as the reservoir was concerned, whether it was ever constructed or not, they kthe Corporation) would take action to compel the Council to take the sewage contamination out of the brook as it interfered with their present working. He then put it to him with regard to the cost of any under- taking, and Mr. Whittle said that if the Connell got out a scheme for taking the sewage out of the brook and submitted it to the Corporation, if approved of, he felt quite confident that the Corporation would help them to a very material extent with the costa of the laying of the pipes from the brook to where it would ultimately fall. Subsequently on the motion of Mr. T Darlington, seconded by Mr. S Woodward, it was decided that the matter be left to the Highway Sub-Committee, together with the Clerk and Surveyor,to report upon when taking their annual round of inspection, and also that the Clerk acd Surveyor of the Warrington Corporation he invited to meet the Council's representatives on the spot. ILLNESS IN AND ABOHT LATCHFORD, A SERIOUS CASE. The Clerk read the following letter :—Gramendale, Fairfield-road. Latchford, Warrington March Mb, 1900. Dear Sir :—With reference to the notice of your last meeting as published in the paper—l do not know whether or not you are cognizant of the fact that there has been serious illness in the houses near mine. There have been two serious cases of diphtheria,and I regret to say that I have lost my son through this. The sickness has without doubt arisen through bad sanitation, cries pools &c. lam informed by Dr. Smith that be reported my ease to your Medical Officer, Dr. Adam, but up to time of writing he has not been near my premises. This, I presume is not an isolated case, judging from the strong remarks made at your last meeting when re-appointing the Medical Officer. There is a sewage rate on my house, and whieh up to the present time has been paid by me, but having no sanitary or sewage arrangement I shall in future protest againet this. I have ssen Mr. Holland, the owner of the houses, and he assures me that as soon as the sewers are laid no time shall he lost on his part in puttine in w.o's. Surely houses at this rental should have better convenience. Trusting that you will have a full and strict investigation made into the serious grievance at once, and much obiige,yours faithfully, W. RUNCORN v. LEEDS PARISH CHURCH.

The Struggle for the 'N.U." Cup.

RUNCORN BEATEN "ON THE POST." - - -

The repiayed tie bet ween Leeds Parish ah—nr—cl; and Runcorn, which took place at Runcorn, oa Wednesday evening, attracted the interest of the whole of the Northern Union football world. Wet unpropitious weather, however, prevented the attendance reaching the recird expected. But nntwithstanding the ill-con- ditions the stream of spectators continued to roll up from an early hour, until at the time of the kick-off there were some 3,000 present. Of late, Runcorn have been performing finely ; the vanquishing of Bradford after two draws was heavy work, and fears were entertained that the meeting with Leeds the following Saturday would sae the °. Linnets " hors de combat. Runcorn's luck, however, proved good, and an uhdoubtedly lucky draw decreed that both should meet again at Runcorn, under more even conditions,to decide wnich club should continue its march towards the cup. ;The home team lackel the services of Warder, Faulkner, Gayter and Darlington. RuncerCa kioked oft against the wind, -an" d Crompton returned into touch near the stands. Tne first advan- tage fell to Leeds right under the Runcorn posts, but Jolley cleared with a fine punt. Half time came two minutes later with the score reading : Leeds Parish Church 1 goal, 1 try (5 points) ; Rencorn 1 try (three points). Butterworth made a lovely opening for Jones who ran round_the posts only to be brought back for some in-

formalitr.- Houghton dropped from c,ntre, but faded. Exciting play-follmed, the Parishioners defendiug stub- bornly. Appointment of Organist and Choirmaster _ _

at Bold-iireet Church.

The appointnanit to the post of organist, and choirmaster in connection with the above place of worshin, rendered vacant by the retirement of Mr Peter Harrison, who had occupied the position for 16 or 17 years was made on Saturday afternoon. Originally there were 25 applicants from all parts of England out of whom six were selected to com- pete. The judge was Mr. Burstall. ANNUAL- MEET-NG OF THE RUNCORN BOATiNG CLUB.

The annual general meting of the abave club was held in the Brunner Guild Hall on Tueeday evening. There was a fair attendance of enthusiastic members. The Secretary (Mr. W. A. Millieger) then submitted his report for 1899 as follows :—The past season, 1899, has been full of interesting events and Mr. Johnson's report will show that we have not exceeded our resources notwithstanding that we have made many improve- ments to our stock and also provided events of interest for our members. During the year we have entered on our books 31 new members. We have been able to pro- vide four new boats, with a total carrying capacity of 12. We have iIiSD during 1899 made many improvements to the boat house, and dressing room, and geaerally im- proved the condition of our stock of boats. Complete toilet requisites have also been provided for the comfort of members. We have also providel at the request of the Weaver Navieation, lamps for the boats out after dark, and I would impress upen members the necessity of.always using these lamps at night time, as we wish to offer the Weaver Authorities as little cause for cumplaint as possible. During the season we ' have also chanced the type of oars for a better make, and I think the oars now supplied are satisfactory. Four new vice-preeidents have been elected, and these gentlemen have very kindly contri- buted to the amount of £4. The draw for :£5 note we had in June was fairly successful and brought in a fair return, and on behalf of the committee I take this opportunity of thanking those members who gave so much energy to disposing of the tickets. It Ices were held in September, and the prizes very kindly given by local gentlemen were krealy contested for. The cup presented by C. N. Irvin I was fortunate enough to secure, and I trust there wet he keen competition this summer as to who will be the holder for 1900. The com- mittee regret exceedingly that generally the events pro- moted for the entertainment of the members have not had their whole hearted support, and in many cases if the committ,e themselves had not supported them some of the items would have fallen fiat,. I trust, how- ever, that our 1900 members will endeavour to exhibit a more enthusiastic and sportsmanlike spirit in the events arraeged by the committee. I much regret to report that (tee of cur old vice-presi- dents has been removed by death during 1899, I refer to Mr. Poilip Speakman of Froisham. This geetlernan has been a supperter of our Club since its inauguration in 1894 up to 1898, and I am sure that in the forwarding of a floral tribute at the time af his interment the Com- mittee only indicated in a slight way the sympathy the members of the Runcorn Boating Club felt for his family in tiatir great bereavement. The dinner held at Wilson's Hotel juet recenty went off well, and had the effect of instilling a closer spirit of comradeship in these members who were not welt-known to the majority. 'Concluding t I think I am simply expressing the wishes of all in wishing for the R. B. C. a long and prosperous life, and if enthusiastic members and enerntic officials will secure it, I an sure that at least for 1900 we shall have a very successful season." Mr. T. Hill said be thought the revert j_leit submitted an excellent one, and one which indicated that during the/past season the committee—backed up by an energe- tic secretary—had made greet strides. He had much pleasure in proposing that the report he passed as sub- na:tteel. Mr. Elandiey seconded with a few well chosen remarks. After long discussion it was proposed by Mr. Bennion that members who had not paid their subscriptions to the club be advised that unless se me were paid by a date to be fixed, their names would be publishtd in the annual report and they would be forbidden the use of the boats for the future. Mr. Hsndley seconded. Carried. Mr H G Johnson Cho treasurer), presented a very lucid report of the club's finances for the year. He said at though they had been put to heavy expense during 1899 he could with much pleasure report a small balance in hand. They had turned over almost £BO. £4O bad been spent on boats,and the rercainderlisad bseu d,sbursed on caretaker's salary, impeovemente, passages. seationery, etc. Profit bad been mede on the draw, dance, dinner, and races, cembireil ; separately' one or two of these events fshewed a deficit. On the whole the year had been a satisfactory one financially. He would have much pleasure is issuing a printed balance sheet when accounts had been euviitr d The 'Treasurer's report which was read in more detail than reported, was unanimously passed, on the proposi- tion of Mr Sherrat, and seconded be Mr Wiswall. The secretary, treasurer, committee, captain and sub- captain were thanked most heartily for :heir excellent services during 1899. 'The election or officers for 1990 vr,,s then proceeded with,and resulted as follows:—Presideet, Sir R M Brooke Bart., J.P. ; vice prffidente t—Sir J T Brunner, Bart., Kee, Mr C N Irven, Mrs liezlehurst,Messre W Timmins, H Baker, J F Hezlehurst, and R U Pesnett ; secretary, Mr W A ; t?easurer, Mr Fl G Johnson ; nape sain, Mr F Abel; euh-ceptain, Mr It Hadfield ; com- mittee, Messrs R Beutlien, G Buxton, F Chrimes, J Speakman, W Harvey, B Joanso», P "uckley, T Bill. The origin of the disease or rather the circumstances which bring it alnut is as much a mystery as ever. You cannot trifle with Influenza,. It undermines the Nervous System to a greater extent than almost any other disease, and it is Pneumonia that kills, and Pneumonia strikes the man who imagines he had only a mild form of Influenza, and can go about the street just as usual. It was a bad day for this Country whea this malignant scourge first visited our shores. Year after year it has never since failed to revive at its due time, and to extort Its tribute of human life. At the Warrington Police Court on Monday, Frederick Littlewood, a e 0-driver residing at 83, Legh-etreet,was charged that he being the driver of a hackney carriage neglected to take to the Police Station within 24 hours a book which he found in his carriage on March 29th— The Chief Constable said that the proceedings were taken under a bye-law of the Borough to the effect that every proprietor or driver of a hackney carriage who should find any property ehould within 24 hours carry the same if it was not claimed by or on behalf of the owners, to the Police Station. On the 29th March a gentle- man named Woolen used the defendant's hao- som. When he got out of the carriage he lett in it his stock-book without which he conic' not do business. On the same day he telegraphed to the Police Station asking for the book to be returned to the Victoria Hotel, Manchester if found. A Police Sergeant imined,atly went to the cab proprietors to g;ve notice and inquire if the book had been found. It had not then been found. A. week later, however, the de- fendant came to the Police Station and handed the book in. The owner had been sadly hampered in his work. It was very important that men should hand in articles found as &Jon as possible.—P.C. Pennington stated that when the defendant brought the book to the Police Station he said that he did not know that found articles had to be brought there within 24 hours.—The Chief Constable said he was surprised at the man com- mitting the offence. He was a very smart, civil and courteous driver. The full penalty was £5 but he thought if the Bench fined the defendant in the costs as It was the first prosecution it would act as an example to other cab drivers and the justice of the case would be met.—Defendant wits fined in the costs which amounted to 3s fid. ALLEGED TREFT OF LEAD AT WINWICIC. —PRISONERS

COMMITTED FOR TRIAL. At the Warrington Occasional Court on Tuesday, before Mr. F. A. Frost, Samuel Warburton and George Ogden were charged with stealing some time between the Ist and 31st of March about 4lewts. of lead of the value of £5, the property of John Jeffery and Company, at Winwick.—Willia.in Porteous, a foreman plumoer in the employ of Messrs Jeffery and Co., at the new asylum works, Winwick, stated that in consequence of something that he heard on the 31st March, he went to the place where the lead was usually kept and found that a great quantity was missing. From there he went to a dung beep on another part of the estate about la quarter of a mile from where the lead was kept, and there fouqd three big rolls of 71b. sheet lead. Be iden- tified it as his masters' property. The value of it was about 17s per cwt. No one had any right to take the lead without his permirsion. The same day he visited the shop of Mr Winstanley, Warrington, in company with P.C. Sharpe and there saw several pieces of lead which he identified as the property of his masters.—Ebenewr Pitchford. a clerk in the employ of William Winstanley, 13, Park-place, Warring- ton, said that on the 21st March a man named Davies came to the shop and sold him 1131119 lead. Be gave him £2 133 9d for it. On the 27th of the same month the man came again and sold him another quantity of lead for which he gave him £3 7s 6d.—James Davies, a rag dealer, residing at 30, King-street, Warrington, said that on the evening 'of the 19th March he met the prisoner Warburton in Wataington. He said he had a lot of scrap lead to sell. After some conservation he agreed to purchase it. The prisoner Ogden was also present. The next morning he went with a ponycart and brought the lead from a midden near a lane in Winwick- It was in bags. He paid the prisoners £2 12s. 6d. and afterwards sold the lead to Mr. Winstanley. On the 26th of the month he again met Warburton who said he had more lead for sale. Next morning he went to the same place and brought some more lead for which he gave 365. The two prisoners came to him again on Friday the 30th March and asked for the loan of a handcart. The next morning he went with them to Winstanley'e shop and obtained a cart for them. They took it away.—?.C. Arthur Sharpe, stationed at Winwick said that at 6.30 .on the Saturday March 3131 he was on duty in the Asylum Grounds. In the course of his rounds he found four pieces of lead hidden by the side of a manure heap 200 yatela from the asylum buildings. Suspecting it had been stolen he watched the place, and at about 9.30 the same morning the two prisoners came with another man whom he did not know with a handcart. They entered the grounds and began to put the lead in the bags when he Came towards them. Immediately they saw him they ran away leaving the handcart, bags and lead. He followed them in the direction of Warrington but lost sight of them At about 5 p.m. on the same Jay he apprehended Warburton in a public-house. He took him to the Police Station and charged him. Re replied " I have not seen that man (meaning Davies) since the Walker affair was on." On the 6th of the present month he arrested on a warrant the man Ogden. He read the warrant to him and he replied "Oh "and after wards said " Well it's got to be proved."—Prisoners were com- mitted for trial at the Liverpool Quarter Sessions.

" SICK OF THE ARMY." —John Seddon was charged with being a deserter from the 3rd Battalion Loyal North Lanca- shire Regiment stationed at Preston since April 7th.—He pleaded gniity.—P.l3. Bell stated that at 6.40 that morning he was on duty in Battersby-lane in company with P.C. Sankey when he noticed the prisoner. He stopped hial and asked him where he was going to and he replied " lam going to Wigan." The man had a military appearance so he asked him if he had a discharge. He said that he had not and subsequently admitted that he deserted on Saturday from the above regiment stationed at Preston.— The Chief Constable said the prisoner told him he was sick of the army. He evidently had no military ardour or ambition —Prisoner was remanded to await an eslort and the Sergeant was recommended for the usual reward.

THE " CELESTIAL " WAY. —The attempt of European ladies to form a league of native girls for the suppression of foot-binding in China has fallen through. One native girl is said to have put the case thus: "We squeezy foot! You squee,zy waist! Same object both—get husband." SOUND ADVICE. —The country editor is a reliable encyclopcedia. A reader sent him this query recently: "What ails my hens? Every morning I find one or more of them keeled over to rise no more." The reply was :—" The fowls are dead; it is a very old complaint, for which nothing eau be done."

A FORMER Duke of Marlborough had some emus in his park. Hence, when the hen bird laid an egg the whole household at Blenheim was full of pleasing excitement. ULduckily, the Duke had just left for London; but a telegram was at once sent after him, which was in the following terms: "The emu has laid an egg, and in the absence of your Grace, we have taken the largesb goose we could find to hatch it." - - _ "WHAT is your line?" brusquely asked the manager of the greatest show on earth. "I believe I am the only middle-aged man in the civilised world," replied the applicant for engage- ment, "who has not said that if he was only a little younger and did not have a family on his hands, he would go to Klondike, and " "I know you are 1" shouted the manager. "Name your price, my dear fellow! Name your price 1" UP A TREE.—A boy was engaged in nocturnal apple-stealing a short tune ago, and was observed by the owner of the fruit, who, unnoticed by the young robber, placed a large stuffed dog at the foot of the tree, and retired to watch the result of his strategy. The boy, about to descend, observed the dog, and then the fun commenced. He whistled, coaxed, threatened, but unavailingly, the animal never moving, and, finally, accepting the inevitable, settled down to pass the night in the tree. After some hours- had elapsed, wearily enough to the lad, morning dawned, and the proprietor of the tree coming from the house asked how he came to be in the tree, to which the boy answered that he took to it to save himself from the dog, which had chased him quite a distance. IDENTIFIED.—The street car struck the rear wheel of the bicycle. The bicyclist described a parabola and fell upon a pile of bricks. The bicyclist raised on one elbow, reached back an arm towards a rear pocket of the knickerbockers, and collapsed, insensible. A sergeant of the police felt in the pocket and drew out a silver cigarette case, marked "J. J. Jones, 400, Bonton Avenue." "Go to 400, Banton Avenue," said the sergeant to a policeman, "and tell Mrs. Jones that Mr. Jones has—" The sergeant paused, and drew a hand- mirror from the other rear pocket of the knicker- bockers. 'Tell Mr. Jones that Mrs. Jones has—" The druggist, who was assisting, tickled the bicyclist's lips w'th .a feather he was trying to burn under the nostres. The bicyclist smiled and murmured, "Charlie !" "Tall Mr. and Mrs. Jones that Miss Jones has met with an accident." How THEY WERE IDENTIFIED. —I was in the grocer's shop this morning, when a woman came in. "I want two dozen eggs," she said to the grocer, "and they must all be laid by black hens." "Madam, it would give me greLt pleasure to serve you, but I must confess that you have got the best of me. I can't tell eg.-{s laid by black hens from any other kind." " i can—inighty quick." "If that's so, I'll let you pick them out." The woman counted two dozen eggs into her basket, and then the grocer rematked, suggestively : " Well, madam, it seems as if the black hens laid all the big eggs." " Yes, that the way you tell 'ern," she replied. How HE KNEW HE HAD LOST HER.—Deep golom and recklessness struggled for supremacy on the expressive countenance of the young man. He fiercely rejected the cup of soothing tea his sister pressed upon him. "It's all up," he an- nounced. "I saw her with that fool Peters again to-day." "Really, that amounts to nothing, my dear boy," said his sister, sympathetically. "A girl may be seen with a dczen men without being engaged to them." "They're engaged all right enough," snarled the unhappy swain. "Why, they were shopping together !" "No matter," persisted the encouraging young woman. "No matter. Don't you give her up—if you really like her, as I'm sure I don't see why you should, for if ever"— "Marion ! Kindly refrain from dis- cussing her !" " All right ; I will. But I was only going to say that you'll be a foolish boy if you give her up just because you happened to see her with that fool Peters in a shop." "They're en- gaged. I could see that," persisted the unhappy one. "How could you? Was it a jeweller's? Was he fitting rings to her fingers?" "No! It was much worse. It was a stove store, and they were pricing gas ranges. Now do you believe ?" And the sceptical sister announced her entire con. version to the engagement theory.

Runcorn Petty Sessions.

MONDLY.—Before Messrs A. Thomas (presiding) and P. Wiewall.

THEFTS OF COAL.—PARENTS SEVERELY CAUTIONED.— Samuel Leigh, Daniel Cavanagh and James Rose, 54. 39. and 47, Gilbert-street, Runcorn, were charged with the larceny of 2 cwts. of coal, valued at is 6d the property of the London and North-We.tern Railway Company. on the 6th of March. —Henry Prescott, stationmaster at ituncorn,said on Tuesday the 6th of March he was on duty at the - station, and saw the three boys on some coal trucks in the dock branch siding. The boys were throwing the coal on both sides of the waggons. He cis led to a porter and they went after the boys. The po-ter caught one of the lads. The proceedings were taken for the purpose of putting a stop to the L practice which was pretty prevelant.—George Robert Blythe. porter in the employ of the Lou ion and North- Western Railway Company remembered the stationmaster calling his attention to the three boys who were on the top of a coal waggon which was standing in the dock branch siding. The boys, who proved lobe the defendants, were throwing coal down from the waggon on to the bank. He (witness) ran down and caught the defendant Rose ; but the other two got clear away. He asked Rose what he had been doing, and be replied he had been putting some coal on the buffer with the intention of taking it home. The lad also stated that his parents had sent him for it. The boys were earrying the coal in their arms, and the lad Rose had a piece of coal weighing about 36 or 40 lbs. when witness caught him. The whole of the coal, that the boys had attempted to take off, when collected and weighed totalled 2 cwts —Constable Thomas S Bowyer swore he had interviews with the boys. separately and collectively. On bath occ sinus they male statements admitting the offence, but said they had not been sent by their parents.—With regard to the bey Rose, the parents had told him they could not do anything with him, and he appeared to be the ring- leader.—The boys pleaded guilty, and their parents electing they should bp summarily dealt with, they were ordered to receive 6 strokes with the birch from Constable Bowyer.— Mr. Thomas, the chairman, spoke very strongly to the parents, and told them that should it be found they were training their children to steal, and they were brought before him for receiving any goods they had known to be stolen, he woula give them all he could. WOKEN Prfeinases.—Emma Woolgar, and Maria Evans. both of Princess-street, Runcorn, were summoned for com- mitting a breach of the peace, by fighting on the 31st inst. Both pleaded guilty.—Constable Gregory said he saw the women fighting in Princess-street. He saw one strike and knock the other down. He then went to them and found they had got hold of eaah other by the hair so tightly that he (witness) had great difficulty in getting them apart.— Both were bound over in 25 to keep the peace for 6 months, and ordered to pay 9s 6d costs.—Maria Evans, 29, Princess- street, Runcorn, pleaded guilty to using obscene language, on the 31st March.—Constable Gregory said this was the same defendant as in the previous case. About quarter of an hour afterwards, he was still in Princess-street. The defendant and her husband were quarrelling about the previous row, and the female was using most obscene language. He told her the would have to stop it. She would not; so he reloaded her.—iined 5s and costs or 14 days. OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Gospel Hall.

On Sunday the speakers ware Messrs. H. Whitehouse and J Elorsefield,tho address being taken from Psalm 37, 7, "Rest in the Lord." Human nature is continually on the look out for rest and satisfaction. To satisfy that craving man seeks natural and worldly pletsures only to find they do not give rest and satisfaction. This can only be found in the One who made man and gave hisn tLis desire. Christ can and does gerfectly satisfy all the cravings of the heart of man. In Christ there is rest from wandering to and fro to find rest. There is rest from foes, human and infernal who would tempt and ensaare. Rest in feeling safe for all Eternity and there test from works to merit salvation. To enjoy this rest 8 the happy privilege of all believers.

- Wycliffe P.S.A. Society. Open Meeting.

Sunday's meeting was thrown open to both sexes and there wou'd be an attendance of about 700. Mr. John Taylor, of Warrington, presided and in a brief address stated that this was an age in which we had more privi- leg a than our forefathers but he serioudy questioned whether the people were as anxious about spiritual life as they should be. They could not live a life of sin and be Christians at the Same thal. It was not consis:ent for a man to live in sin and go under the cloak of a Chris- tian.—Major Ballantine, of Menchester, in the course of an address said he was delighted to see such a large gathering because it showed that the work of the society was being carried on successfully,and from what he had ascertained from the secretary he uncleratood that the actual and practical results were very gratifying. As Christians he was afraid they did nct do as much work in winning souls to Cnrist as they might. If they di more in that direction England would be a far differaw: nation. It was for them to go on and" not be weary in well doing." The Enelish were a practical people, and in their philanthropic efroreA they must be practical. Many o.! the philaatbropie soci, ties over-lapped each other and a great deal of philanthropy was frittered away through falsesentiment. Their philanthropic efforts needed to be wisely directed, and placed in the right hands if they were to be of any avail. In conclusion the speaker referred to the chief aim of man, stating that it was to glorify God.—The Rev J. Yonge announced that a petition in favour of prevent- ing the sale of drink to children was open for the signa- ture of members. He stated that it was a most impor- tant measure, and one which stood a good chance cf passing if Christian people generally, and workingmen particularly, would throw their influence into the scale. --Mrs. R. H. Pearson very sweetly sang two solos entitled " When Thy children fall asleep " and " Jerusalem."—Mr. W. H. Payton, Mug. Bic., presided at the organ. The service was also taken part in by Mr Beswick and Mr. Scriven.

Generous "Tommy."

The generosity of " Tommy " is well shown in a com- munication from a phategrapher at the front to the photographic journal " Photography." In the course of an intereFting letter he points out that where men are killed their kits are sold for the benefit of their wives and families. The kits of married men whwe families remain at home have been sold at quite startling prices. As instances, an old kit bag, value when new about 91., fetch 255., a pair of laces, value id., fetched 54., and he reports that the bidding was quite brisk and ex- citing, plenty of heart being put into it. " Single men's kits are quite unsaleable ; no one wants the things, ex- cept the shirts and socks, and no one will bid, so it is quite clear that these fancy prices are only a means oE contributing to the widows in a manner which has no appearance of charity, and deserves, therefore, to be much more commended. I should add that many of the men who offer these prices only receive a is. a day." The knowledge of facts like these ought to help the stria- scription lists not a little.

NORTHERN UNION CUP COMPETITION. FOURT-1-1-R-OUND. Salford v. i-3-W.:1;le Hornets.

The game to play off the tie between those two clubs ' on the Salferd 4round, at New Barns, was seen by up- wards of 12,000 people. The teams were at full strength and an open and interesting game was played. There was not much to choose between the two sets of for- wards, bet the Salford backs outpaced their opponents and combined more effectively, with the r, sult that they throughout appeared to advantage, and ultimately the Hornets were defeated by eleven points to three. Sal- ford lust the toss for choice of ground and played up the slope in the first half but with the wind. The Hornets were soon forced back, T Williams, intercepting a pass by Kruger and running to their quarter. Eventually Smith, the Salford full back, punted the ball over the goal line, and it rolled dead at the hack of the posts. Soon after drorping out from the quarter mark Smith followed up a fine return kick and tackled the opposing full back near the Hornets' goal line. Relief was soon afferded, and Rigg and Wightman, with a fine dribble, forced Salford to concede a minor point. Good pascing by the tome backs soon again placed the Horrets on the deiensive and at length fine combination by the two halves, Thomas, T Williams, and Pearson, enabled the last-named player to secure an unconverted try for Salford. • The visitors then pressed and forced Salford to save. Rigg made a good but unsuccessful attempt at a penalty goal and Wightman just missed a try by having the ball kicked (1,84 as he was falling on it. Near the close of the first half Had- wen secured the second Salford try, but Griffiths failed at goal. At half time Salford led by two tries to nil. On resuming play, the game was continued for a time without either side gaining any tangible advantage. At lenc..th Kruger kicked the ball towarde the Salford goal line, and Rigg, following up smartly, gained possession and secured an unconverted try. This reverse appeared to rouse the home players. Brown made a mark in the Hornets' quarter and the ball was returned into touch, whence Griffiths punted it in front of the goal posts and J Williams secured a try for Salford, Brown placing an eacy goal. There was no further scoring and "no side" found Salford victorious by one goal and three tries ((leven points) to one try (three points).

===== 0002601_19000412_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
I Swinton v. Broughton Rangers.

There was a very large attendance ot spectators to witness thig tie, which was played at Swinton, the ground being packed. Both clubs were compelled to make changes in their teams through the enforced absence of Stickney on the Broughton Rangers side and Lewis of Swinton, their places being taken by Duck and J. Valentine respectively. The play was not of a very brilliant character, while the tackling was most vigorous end at time?: bordered upon roughness, and the referee, Mr. M'Cutcheon, was compelled upon more than one eecesion to caution players on both sides. While the Swintonians relied upon the combination of their backs Cc' gain a victory,the Rangers ape eared to play the rush- ing and high kicking game, and few combined runs,with tile exception of several between the brothers James, were made. Swinton started the game, facieg a strong wind, and ho h ends in turn were quickly visited. A fine run between Davies, Morgan, and J. Valentine nearly ended in the latter player getting through. W. James tried to drop a goal,but the ball just went outside Ihe post, and halt-time neither side bed gained a major point. Upon changing ends the Swintonians were cpeirkly the aggressors, and pressed their opponents very hard on the goal line. At length Hampson,after a smart passing run, rushed over and gained Swinton's first try. From this point of the game the home players showed to advantage ; indeed, only upon one occasion afterwards did !he Rangers appear dangerous, when Chorley, who had played a fine game, snapped up the ball on his goal line and cleared it to midfield. Messer gain( d two more tries for Swinton by clever running and dodging, but heth place kicks failed. The final score was :—Swinton, three tries (nine points) ; Broughton Rangers, nothing.

At Edgeley Park, Stockport, before 6,000 spectators. 'Stockport, generally held th s upper hand, but found the define° of Widnes very safe, and there was no scfore at half-time. trttr3 to the Obiter.

Some Curious Phenomena of the War.

S.r,—Tise war n,, rri in Soeth Affirm has given ris t some curious and suggestive phenomena upon which, wito r ur Kind permission I should like to offer a little free and independent criticism. For a non siderable time prior to the breaking out of hostiltiese not a few prominent politicians were engaged in making vehement speeches, both in Parlia- ment and out of it, aeainet. this country going to war with the Tzaosvaal Republic. Wheteor those speeches were mere rhetorical ll:eurishos to catch the public ear, or genuine expreisione of well grounded conviction I cannot say. But a very grave fact in relation to them stands out clear and distinct before the world. When the speakers found that their eloquence was powerless to arrest the bane of the goeerernent they imentdiately turned round and stultified themselves by voting for the men and moues- demand° I for the pro?ecutieu of a war which they had just denounced as unjustifiable and crinainal. One cannot reset the conviction that such conduct WA9 dishonourable to educated men, and be- trayed a looseness of principle up)n a momentous sub- ject that will assuredly lower them in the oyes of honest people. In mist spheres of humble life such double dealing would cause men to be distrusted and shanned by their friends and neighbours. At the outset, and for a long time after, there was a great and bewildering confusion regarding the object of the war. SAM held that England was fighting for equal rights all iourie in the Transvaal; others were of opinion that the Neheth'e vineyard of the goldfie'ds Was the end in view ; weile seme, more profane than the rest, de- clared from the pulpits that the killing and wounding of the Boers would tend to the e xtension of Christ's King- dom ! But ell doubt and uncertainty upon the subject leave been cle.ared up now. The Transvaalers and their friends are fighting for liberty and independence—two very precioua things to brave and self respecting people —but our object is to deprive them of both. and to put a foreign yoke round their necks. Now I venture to submit that a war of this kind, a war of the strong against the weak, of a mighty empire again.t people not more numerous than the inhabitants of Ssliord is net one to commend itself to just and humane minds. Besides such a war has never before been known in our history,and its inception and carrying out aresure indica- tions of national declension from the ancient traditions of English justice and fairplay. But in whatever light we may regard the war there can be no room fez doubting its popularity. It has raised a greet wave of enthusiasm all over the country. Liberals and Conservatives, Mayors and Town Council- lors, religious people, and people of no religion are all clamourine for its continuance to the bitter end, that is to the aneihilation of the Boer Republics. All this muet be discoureglng to the friends of peace whe are now des- pised on all hands, some of them even suffering insult and violence on account of their advocacy of peace prin- ciples. But surely no reasonable person will deny that public opinion, however energetically expressed is, at the best, hot a very fallible standard by which to test the righteousness of any cause. Men are naturally fickle, and more easity led by wrong than by right influences, applauding one day and condemning the next. The Saviour of the world had a painful experience of the shortsightedness and perversity of our poor human nature. The crow& who, on a memoral le occasion, escorted him to Jerusalem with shouts ofshosanna were in all probability, the very men who, a few days after, cried out crucify Him, ci ucify Him. And it may be that some of the au' hors of this popular war will live to see the day wire, its awful cost of blood and treasure having been brought home to the putlic mind, they will be execrated as enemies of their country. But we have a higher and a curer test than public opinion to apply te the war, and according to this test its popularity is the strongest condemnation that could be brought against it. The test te• which I refer is to be found in the solemn words ef Jesus, namely, "That which is highly esteemed &moue men is abomination in the sight of God." (Luke xvi., 15). I remember the war in the Crimea, but I cannot call to mind that anything was done by the public to miti- gate the sorrow and destitution of the widows and or- phans of the men who perished in that terrible conflict. But we seem to have become more humane now. Some time ago tbe Queen ordered her faithful henchmen, the Bishops, to make collections in all the State Churches for the wives and children of the auxiliary forces "called to the front." And even before tier Majesty took the matter in hand local bodies were getting up subscriptions for the same purpose. Now it seems to me that this is rather a shabby way of shifting responsibility from the proper shoulders. If the House of Commons found no difßculty in voting sixty millions sterling ior fighting purposes surely it might have provided a few millions to keep the dependents of the soldiers from starvation ; more especially as it knew that multitudes of these dependents would be made widows and orphans by the operations of the war. But if the Government is mean enough to allow some of the families of its wor paid servants to depend upon the cold hand of public charity for subsistence it is the rich and not the poor that should be called upon to contribute the funds. The people who possess the wealth of the country can a'.;ford to be patriotic ; but it is neither fair nor seemly to pass the colleetion sheets into factories and other centres of labour where conaparatively few of the worLers can get beyond " a living wage." One striking point more ere 1 close. The great enthu- siasm evoked by the war has not been without its wet blankets. And these cooling appliances were ad- ministered by no ism a personage than the Chancellor of the Exchequer. War costs money,as well as human and animal lives, and the present one is being waeed at an enormous expenditure of both. The Finance Minister has, of courss, to make the payments and he must look se the taxpayers for the necessary cash. No sr it is surely reasonable to expect that the people who "delight in war" sleeted also delight in paying for it. But this is not always the case. In the preeent instance, directly it became known that the Chancellor's Budget contained additional taxation on account of the war, there was a tremendous rush to take the dutiable articl s involve I out of bond in order that they might escapethe advanced itnposts ! The friends of peace are too few and too poor to have paid two millions in a few days for such a pur- pose. It was the "Big Englanders," the people who love to aillg. " Rule Britannia " and Kipling's doggerel. who rushed to the Customs to save their pockets ! Yours truly, April 6;11, 1900.

WARRINGTON FOOTBALL CLUB.

The Works' Competition.

The matches in the Works' Competition have been con- tinued during the past week, and seine interesting games have been witnessed. On Tuesday evening the first round was brought to a close with a finely-contested game between the C.L.C. 3nd Messrs. itylencia' Bros. Monday's evening's match was a game between Rich- mond's and Edeisten's.A fine evening and the prospect of a close struggle drew a good number te the Wilderspool enclosure. Nothing could be more lauehable to the real football enthusiast than to see the floundering about and crude atteinpts at "style" that are witnessed in connection with these works' cempetitions. Toe jerseys too, are of every conceivable cescription and contain as many colours as the rainbow. Monday's match was some. thing of this kind 8 ei a score was indicated by a blast on an unmusical horu. The best display of football was given by the winoing eide who showed the better com- bination. In the second half, however, the railway men showed superier stamina and used their addiLioaal weight with effect. The Rylands',team had EcircAy a chance in this balf, and the railway men ran out winners by three goals, three tries (15 wenn+) to nil.

THE WAR.

LATEST TELEGRAMS.

Activity of the Boers. Safe Return of a Missing Patrol.

Telegram from Lord Roberts.

The following has been posted at the War Office from Lord Roberts:—

Bloemfontein, April 10.—The euemy have been very active during the pat few days.

One commando is now on the north bank of the Orange River, not far from Aliwal North, while another is attacking Wepener. The garrison there is holding out bravely, and inflicted serious loss on the Boers yesterday. Major Springer, of the Cape Mounted Rifles killed. No other casualties reported as yet. The troops are being moved up rapidly. Patrol of six men of the 7th Dragoons reported missing since the night of April 7th.

From Lord Roberts to the Secretary of State for VFar:--

• Bloemfontein, April 10.—Patrol reported missing in my telegram to-day has returned safely.

Alleged Serious Disaster, 600 Killed and Wounded. 900 PRISONERS. Boer Loss Small.

A" Daily Mail" telegram from Brandort says that General De 14 et on Saturday inflicted a third defeat on the British within a week, at Meerkats- fontein, killing and wounding 600. He captared 009 prisoners, and twelve waggons, losing five Boers killed and nine wounded. The "New York Herald" Paris correspondent ha 3 received a telegram from Preturia as follows:— Pretoria, Sunday.—De Wet continues his brilli- ant operations south of Bloemfontein. Yesterday he again inflicted a blow upnn the British attacking them near Meerkatsfoutein. He captured 900 prisoners and iuflicted heavy losses. The remainder of the British retreated towards Bloemfontein. De Wet's flying column has within a week defeated the British twice, captured 1,800, seven guns, and 'extensive booty.

A" Daily News" telegram fiom Pretoria is to the same effect, but gives 800 prisoners.

[lt will be seen that Lord Roberts's despatch, written three days after this alleged engagement, makes no mention whatever of any such event.]

The Fighting at Mafeking. Coronel Pi umer's Wound.

In the action between Ramathlabama and Male- king, on March 31, Colonel Plumer and Major Weston were slightly wound ed. The other casual- ties are comparatively heavy.

No More Leniency for Cape Rebels.

A War ning Proclamation.

Lord Roberts has issued a proclamation warning the inhabitants of Nortk ern Cape Colony that the utmost rigours of martial law will be shown offenders aiding the enemy.

THE EXAMINER, THURSDAY, APRIL 12. 1900.

1900. Dear Father,—l received the papers and letter all I right to-day, and also one from W--, who tells me he is nice and comforteb'e. By the time you get thii I darpF.ay y•-•u will have c,ylv hrgotten hoot the capture (if Ci,ntrai Crnnj;) and 4,000 er 5,000 prisoners Sze., but it was we and the Canadians who ca)4ht them.Tll,l7 Lad tons of ammunition, and, as I tol I you, e ere al- ways sniping at Us, although only one of our company was hit through the leg, but not very seriously. ‘Ve are still in the same place. It is raining nearly every night. We are flooded out, but there is nothing else for it but lie and sleep in it. It is very nice in the day. I have got the choeolate box with me ready to send to you or W----(that is, it con would like it) as soon as we get to a station. I can'i-, srid it from here as it wou'd get lost. We can't get stamps or anything else here, as wa are in tha country. We are still living on three biscuits a day, and meat. We cannot eat the meat as it is tough. We have all gone short of tobacco and we cannot buy any. The other day some of the men were giving 103 for a few cigarettes, although tobacco is the cheapest thing we can buy here, for it is only is 41 a pound to us, and good 'bacca. too. We have smoked all theßoer tobacco now, and can't buy a bi', I supoose, till we get to some station,—From your loving SOO,FRED, wishing you all good luck." Added to this is a post- script which roads as follows :—"One half of the Volun- teers will be sorry, I'll bet,if they get on the same terms. There would not be so many willing to come out here if they knew what they would have to undergo."

A Warrington Policeman with Lord

Roberts.

The following letter has been received by P.C. Hesp, of the Warrington Police Force, from P.C. S. Howard, a member of the force, who is serving in South Africa with the 3rd Grenadier Guards :—" 3eci Grenadier Guards, South A trice, March Bth, 1900. Dear Ted,—l now take the opportunity of writing yoe a few lines in answer to your letter es hich I did not reeeive until the 2611 of February. I was very pleased to hear from you and my comrades. I expect you are having some good news in England now the tide hes turned and we are making the Boers fly in every direction. We have left Lor Methuen's Division. It is bi.eken up and he is in Kimberley. We are with Lord Roberts fiehting our way to Bloemfontein the capital of the Free State and from which we are about 25 miles distant. Roberts is a good general and we are only to ) will- ing to do anything he puts us to de. About a week ago our battalion escorted some 4,000 Boer 'prisoners from Slip Drift to Modder River. They were nothing but a lot of dirty rebels. There were women with children in their arms about five or snx menths Some of them I darosay having been on the field since the war started. A great many or the aisti would be about 80 years of age. Don't be aexious to come out here Ted as there is a let of marching to do in the blazing sun. 1 had bliiters on my feet as big as camp kettles. Though so hot in the day time it is ye y cold at night and sometimes rain, in (orrecits all night through. We have no shelter only what we are standing up in not even a loose coat. There are a great many dead Boers, oxen, and mules, lying on the road and the smell is enough to give one the fever. We bad a battle about two days ago. The Boers retreited and we captured two guns. They left them on the top of a kopje not being able to get them away without risking capture themselves. A Boer prisoner told ma tne other day that we should never get into Bloemfontein or Pretoria but I told him we should or die in the attempt. The men who work the Boors big guns are mostly Germans or French- men; they do not knee much about them t hemiel yes but profess to use the rifle and are always tllking about their shooting. Although we are suffering a lot of hardships, Jack (P.C. 33 John W Hurst) and I are in as good health as ever and he wishes to be remembered to you all. I don't think we will want any more troops out here. We have only to give the Boers a good thrashing so if all gees on well Gibbs, Bradley and yourself will not be sent out to give us a hand. I received tho paper 1.113V;i8 sent about an hour ago and I was very pleased to get it. Hurst and I send our bast wishes to all on the force. This is all I have to say at present from your old pal, Samuel Howard."

MILITARY NOTES.

Second Lieutenant Broadbent lately appointed to the .Ist Volunteer Battalion South Lancashire Regiment, at Warrington, has been appointed lieutenant in the Imperial Yeomanry and has lett for the front. Second Lieutenant Stuart of the 3rd Battalion South Lancashire Regiment in headquarters at War- ringtou,has been given a commission in the regular service becoming Second Leutenant in the Man- chester Regiment. He will join the new 3rd bat- talion of the corps now being formed. He was ap- pointed Second Lieutenant in the 3rd battalion South Lancashire Rogiment in November last. Captain Lally Flood just promoted to that rank in the South Lancashire Regiment, being employed with the mounted infantry in South Africa there is now a vacancy for a captain in the regiment which will probably be tilled by the promotion of Lieu- tenant Carlyou.

Major Fre-wen who was a short time ago brigade adjutant of the 9th Yeomanry Cavalry brigade which consists of the Cheshire Yeomanry and the Lancashire Hussars, has been promoted lieu- tenant-colenel and appointed to command the 16th Lancers now on active service in South Africa.

Second Lieutenant Musgrave, lately appointed to the Arms, Service Cmps from the sth Battalion Sings Liverpool Regiment, with headquarters at Warrington, has been appointed to do duty with the A.S.C. at London

Lieutenant Fox has been promoted Captain in the Liverpool Regiment, and takes over command of a Company in the Ist Battalion in south Africa Captain Greenway will also shortly be promoted to the rank of Major in the Liverpool Regiment, and then Lieutenant Denham of the 2nd Battalion, and who is serving at Warrington with the Depot Com- panies of the Corps, will be advanced to the rank of Captain in the Liverpool Regiment. Second-Lieutenant Radford appointed to a com- mission in the Liverpool Regiment from the Derby- shire Militia Battalion at Manchester, will join the new 3rd Battalion Liverpool Regiment now being formed. Both the 3gd and 4th Battalions Liverpool Regiments are being strengthsned by recruits from the depot at Warrington but at present both these Battalions have a strength of only about 200 of all ranks for the two battalions.

FOREIGN MISSIONARY ANNI-

VERSARY.

Services and Meetings at Warrington.

Foreign missionary anniversary services were held in Bold-street Wesleyan Chapel on Sunday. In the morn- ing the Rev C. H. Hocken (late of India) preached a sermon from the words " Decei.re Ilia glory among the heathen, hi a worders among all people." Psalm xevi.3. In the course of his remarks he spoke of the religious life of the Hindoos, and gave examples of the tortures and punishments they.inflicted upon themselves in order that, as they thought, they might be cleansed frern their sins. Fr instanca,a Hindu woman would walk through jungles, forests, over rivers, enceuntering risks of being robbed, in order to wash herself iu the waters of tee sacred river. A male Hindu would sit in a ring of fire with his head shaved exposed to tne heat of a burning Indian sun for the same object. The rev. gentlemen eehibited a picture which he had houelit, outside a tem- ple, which showed the Hinclu conception of Goef,and an idol which was called the God of Difficulty. It was a man's body with an elephaut's hesd and four arms. The heathens had unworthy and inaleqeste conceptions of God, and the great aim of the m'ssionaries was to per- lect these conceptions by the teachines of Christianity . The missionaries told them of things that their best and highest teachers had been dreaming ahout and feeling afcer in the dark—thiegs that they might discover in Christ.

A service somewhat novel in character was held in the schoolroom in the afternoon, presided over by Mr Prince Mr and Mrs Hoc'len and Master Hocken (seven years of age)aopqaredion the platform attired as a El indx) family. Mrs Hocken sang some Kanaresv lyrics, and her husband, from his own rxperience in India of the famine, gave a description of the misaionary's care for the " famine orphan." He produc4d portraits arirsome of the little rescued ones, and Master Hocken spoke a little in the Kanarese language. There was a large coneTegation at the eveoirra ser- vices, when Mr. Hocken preached from the words "And ye shall be witnesses unte Me both in Jerusalem, and in Judea, an in Samaria. and unto the uttermost parts of the earth."—Acts i. 8. The speker urtred upon the congrega,ion to become " witnesaes," so that, by their live 4, they might win others for the Lord. He showed how the disciples understood the last instructions of the Saviour and carried them out, and if they were men endowed with the grace of God there was no option left but to folic w thrir example. He pointed out in goriclusion, the great ne,fis o the heathen world, and appealed to the corgregation to take their side in the grt at campaign for God and for righteoumesa against all iniquity.

The holidays this year in Earlestowit and Newton will be similar to those of previous Eastertides. All the works will close on Thursday evening, and re- open on Tuesday morning next. There are no local attractions announced with the exception of a foot- ball match between Earlestown and Prescot, when the Earlestown bend will be in attendance, and doubtless this will attract a good number of young people for the subsequent dancing. It is pretty cer- tain a large number of people will leave the town for the popular resorts, and full advantage will be taken by the employes at the Viaduct Works of the privilege of travPlling at quarter fare. The Railway Company also offer facilitie, for reaching Liverpool, Manchester and other places at reduced rates. LORD AND LADY NEWTON.

The park is one of the most picturesque and shiest in England, end deer have roamed in it for nearly 600 years. Lady Newton was a Miss Bromley-Daven- plrt, daughter of the late Mr. Bromley-Davenport. of Capesthorne,nEar Crowe, and a sister of the /re- sent owner of the property, who is so p3pular in society, also such a first-rate amateur actor, and who is now in South Africa with the Staffordshire Yeomanry. Lord Newton only succeeded to the title last rar, and before that was for some time Conservative member for Newton in Lancashire.

ST. HELENS.

A Sr. FIRGENE BOY'S STRANGE DEATEr. At the St. Helens Town Hall on Monday Mr. S. Brig- house, county coroner, held the inquest on the body of James Houltram, aged twelve years, of 62, Union-street, who died on Thursday night last under most extraor- dinare circumstances. The deceased's mother said that on Sunday afternoon, the Ist inst., the boy Callls horns and said a lad had struck him on the head. Ee did not say who it was, nor where it happened. He was crying but soon stope ed. The boy did not go to school on Monday afternoon or Tuesriay. On Thursday he began vomiting it seven o'elock in the morning. About half past six on Thursday night, when lying on the sofa, she hoard If m making a strange noise. and before the doctor could be brought he died.—Dr. Unsworth who had made a post-mortern, said there were no marks of violence on the lad. A piece of grewth had been detached from the surface of one of the glands, and had been carried into the brain, stopping the circulation in the brain. Death was primarily due to heart disease, and secondarily to embollism.—The:Coroner said this was a very interesting case to a doctor. They could get no evidence whatever as to tho allPaed blow on the head.—A verdict of "Natural causes" was returned.

WARRINGTON.

P.S A. at the Y.M.C.A.—The Late Private

Blackwood.

The weekly meeting of the above society was held in the Lecture Hall, which was crowded oil Sunday afternoon, the president (Mr. W. Hickman) presiding. An interesting address was delivered by Mr Arthur Mounfield on "Seeing and not seeing." Twa appr)priate solos, "The Last Muster," and "He shall wipe the tear from every eye," were efficiently rendered by Mrs Gleave, and a vote of condolence was passed in silence with the 'parents and friends of the late Private W Blackwood, one of the P.S.A. members'. who recently died of pneu- monia at the front. The proceelings throughout were of a vary impressive character. Presentation at Lymm.

On Monday evening a social gathering was held in the Wesleyan schoolroom, Lymm, when the op- portunity was taken of making presentations to Mr and Mrs James Dodds, of Crooklands, f.yrnm, who are about to leave to take up their residence at l'enketh. Mr Dodds replied in suitable terms on behalf of Mrs Dolds and himself. During the evening a musical programme was provided by several friends.

One of the largest gates seen at an A.fsaciation match witnessed the meeting of these Mid-Cheshire organise- I Mons at Warrington on Seterday. HOUSE-BREAKING. _ A Warrington Girl's Escapade.

Concealing a Thief in the Coal-Hole.

At the Warrington Police Court on Wednesday, before Mr. Birchall (in the chair) and Councillor Smethurst, Emma Morecroft,a girl of 19, was charged with stealing from the dwellinghouse No. 67. Lengford-street, some- time between 6.15 p.m. on the 21st January and 11 a.m. on the 24th January, two teapots and £6 in money, the property and moneys of Thomas Hardman.—She pleaded floe Chief Constable stated the facts nf the cage.
